## Preface.

Well-disposed, art-loving <note>In this context, "art-loving" refers to those interested in the "Hermetic Art," another name for alchemy.</note> reader! What the wise man Sirach <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) in the Apocrypha, specifically the section known as the "Praise of the Ancestors."</note> says in the 44th Chapter: Let us praise famous men: They have counseled wisely, and left behind honorable names and glorious writings: They were also rich, and possessed great wealth, and their praise shall not perish. They are buried in peace, but their name lives forever. The people speak of their wisdom, and the community proclaims their praise; This may not improperly be said of the true **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis." In early modern texts, "philosopher" often specifically refers to alchemists who seek natural and divine wisdom.</note> and **chemists** <note>original: "Chymicis." These were the practitioners of "Chymia," a field then encompassing both what we now call chemistry and alchemy.</note>, inasmuch as they have indeed spoken wisely, and left behind honorable names and glorious writings: They were also rich, and possessed great wealth, and their praise shall not perish. They are buried in peace, but their name lives forever. The people speak of their wisdom, and the community proclaims their praise.

Since their name is to live forever, and the community shall proclaim their praise; Therefore I am

<summary>This preface introduces an alchemical or scientific treatise by quoting the Book of Sirach to justify the honoring of famous scholars. The author aligns the "true philosophers and chemists" with the biblical tradition of wise, wealthy, and eternally remembered men.</summary>
<keywords>Sirach, alchemy, chemistry, philosophers, preface, fame, wisdom, early modern science</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page: "Therefore I am..." The catchword "be-" on page 3 matches the first word "bewogen" (moved) on this page.</meta>

moved for the following reasons to promote the printing of these three small treatises.

First, so that God’s great wisdom and wonders might be further spread, and His name praised through them, according to the words of the wise man: My lips shall praise you when you teach me your wisdom. Afterward, I will proclaim your wonders, so that others also turn to you, and they shall praise your name forever and ever.

Second, out of the love I bear for the sons of learning and wisdom, but especially and most of all, so that the simple-hearted <note>Refers to honest seekers who lack formal academic training and are thus vulnerable to scams.</note> may be warned from a faithful heart against sophists and deceivers. Furthermore, I wish for the **Philosophical Rose Garden** <term>Philosophical Rose Garden: A common metaphor in alchemy representing the collection of wisdom and the process of spiritual and material transformation.</term> to be further cultivated and adorned with beautiful, lovely foreign plants (by which I mean writings translated from foreign languages into our mother tongue).

Lastly, as an expression of gratitude and to ensure a lasting legacy, which is owed to these famous people—the **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis"</note> who are our **preceptors** <note>original: "Præceptoribus"</note> and master teachers—so that their praise may never vanish and their names may remain forever.

Now that God, through His great love and mercy, has enlightened my understanding (for which I thank Him with heart and mouth), so that I have drawn the knowledge of the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone: The legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life or spiritual perfection.</term> from the writings of the philosophers through diligent reading and deep reflection...

<summary>The author outlines three reasons for publishing these alchemical works: glorifying God, protecting students from fraudulent "sophists," and honoring the ancient masters of the craft. He concludes by crediting divine mercy and hard study for his own understanding of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, translation, education, religious justification, sophistry, Philosophical Rose Garden</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's justification for publishing these works, picking up from the previous page's discussion on honoring the "master teachers" of alchemy. The catchword "Denn" (For) connects the flow of the argument regarding the purpose of books.</meta>

Therefore, I consider it a duty that one should not let the good books of the wise perish, but rather bring them to posterity through repeated printing; for books are vessels of memory and an everlasting name for the sages. Furthermore, if God’s great wisdom and wonders are to be spread ever wider, then the writings and books of the ancient sages must indeed be sought out and promoted for public printing, so that the secret wisdom and truth of the ancients, which lies hidden in their writings, may be revealed and made known to the sons of wisdom.

But because the true understanding of this high **secret** <note>original: "Secreti"</note> can hardly—or perhaps not at all—be drawn from books alone through mere reading, without the Spirit of God (in whose hand everything stands); I wish above all things to diligently and earnestly admonish the researchers and disciples of this holy art and science to prayer. For prayer is the true master-key to obtain grace from God, that He might take away the darkness and blindness of our hearts, so that our understanding may be enlightened. Through our asking, seeking, and knocking, we may be granted to find the true **physical keys** <term>Physical keys: In alchemical literature, these are the specific chemical agents or laboratory processes required to "unlock" or transform matter.</term> which unlock all the gates of the **secrets of nature** <note>original: "Geheimnüssen der Natur"</note> and can show and reveal to us the **healing well of health** <term>Healing well of health: A reference to the "Universal Medicine" or Elixir, believed to cure all diseases and prolong life.</term> and wealth.

<summary>The author argues that publishing ancient alchemical texts is a moral duty to preserve wisdom and glorify God, while emphasizing that true understanding requires both study and earnest prayer to receive divine enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>preservation of knowledge, divine wisdom, prayer as a key, secrets of nature, physical keys, healing well of health, sons of wisdom</keywords>

(4.)

...I have been moved for the sake of the following reasons to promote the printing of these three little treatises.

First, so that God's great wisdom and wonders might be further spread, and His name praised thereby, according to the statement of the wise man:
> My lips shall praise You when You teach me Your wisdom. Thereafter I will proclaim Your wonders, so that others may also convert to You, and they shall praise Your name forever and ever. <note>This is a paraphrase of biblical passages, specifically combining elements of Psalm 119:171 and Psalm 51:13.</note>

Secondly, out of the love I bear for the sons of learning and wisdom; but especially and most of all, so that the simple-hearted <note>The "simple-hearted" refers to honest but perhaps uneducated seekers who are easily misled.</note> might be warned from a faithful heart against the sophists and deceivers, and also that the <term>Philosophical Rose Garden</term> <note>A common alchemical metaphor for a collection of wisdom or a specific state of the alchemical process; here it refers to the body of literature itself.</note> might be increasingly cultivated and adorned with beautiful, lovely foreign plants (by which I mean writings translated from foreign languages into our mother tongue).

Finally, as a testimony of gratitude and for a good legacy which is owed to famous people—to the philosophers as our <term>Preceptors</term> <note>original: "Præceptoribus," meaning teachers or tutors.</note> and master teachers—so that their praise may never perish and their name may remain eternally.

Now that God, through His great love and mercy, has enlightened my understanding (for which I thank Him with heart and mouth), so that I have drawn the knowledge of the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and acting as a universal medicine.</note> from the writings of the philosophers through diligent reading and deep reflection,

<meta>This page continues the author's introductory justification for publishing these alchemical texts. The opening word "Therefore" (Als) connects the previous page's reasoning—honoring teachers and warning seekers—to the duty of preserving knowledge through the printing press.</meta>

(5.)

Therefore, I consider it a duty that one should not allow the good books of the wise to perish, but rather bring them to posterity through repeated printing; for books are vessels of memory and the eternal names of the wise. If God’s great wisdom and wonders are to be spread ever further, then indeed the writings and books of the ancient wise must be sought out and brought to public print, so that the secret wisdom and truth of the ancient sages, which lies hidden in their writings, might be revealed and made known to the sons of wisdom. <note>The "sons of wisdom" (Söhne der Weißheit) is a traditional term for those initiated into the study of alchemy and hermetic philosophy.</note>

However, because the true understanding of this high **secret** <term>secret</term> <note>original: "Secreti." In an alchemical context, this refers to the *Arcanum* or the hidden core of the Great Work.</note> can hardly, or perhaps not at all, be drawn from books alone through mere reading without the Spirit of God (in whose hand everything stands), I wish, above all things, to diligently and earnestly exhort the seekers and disciples of this holy art and science to prayer. For prayer is the true master key to obtaining grace from God, so that He may take away the darkness and blindness of our hearts, that our understanding might be enlightened, and that through our asking, seeking, and knocking, we may be granted to find the true **physical** <term>physical</term> <note>original: "physicalische." While we today associate "physical" with physics, to an early modern alchemist it referred to "Physick" or natural philosophy—the actual, material manipulation of nature's laws.</note> keys, which unlock all the gates of the secrets of nature and can show and reveal to us the healing fountain of health and riches.

...ck. <note>This completes the word "unlock" from the previous page.</note> For whoever tirelessly bends his back / and has the desire to read our books / says Baccaser in the *Turba* <note>original: "Turba." Refers to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the most famous and influential early alchemical texts.</note>, and applies himself to it with all diligence / uses his understanding for it / directs his mind toward it / and is not burdened with idle and earthly / indeed, not at all useful thoughts / and besides this asks God the Almighty / as Solomon did / for wisdom / and not for money and goods (like a **Sophist** <term>Sophist</term> <note>In alchemical writing, a "Sophist" is a derogatory term for a "false alchemist" or "puffer"—someone who seeks only material wealth and lacks the spiritual or philosophical foundation of the Art.</note> and one greedy for money / whose soul thirsts much more for gold / than for God and wisdom) he shall without ceasing rule and govern in our kingdom as a king / until his end.

Because also / according to the statement of *Brother Basil Valentine* <note>original: "Fratris Basilii Valentini." A legendary alchemical figure, supposedly a Benedictine monk, whose works emphasize the spiritual and chemical properties of antimony and the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>, the doors to the ancient **Stone of the Wise** <term>Stone of the Wise</term> <note>original: "Stein der Weisen." Also known as the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting long life.</note> / and to the inexhaustible fountain of health / are so firmly barred and locked / and among hundreds of thousands / who run after this noble jewel / scarcely two or three attain it; Therefore it must indeed be highly necessary / that alongside diligent and thoughtful reading of good books / as well as work arranged according to nature / dear prayer also not be set aside.

One should also know / that / although the **Matter** <term>Matter</term> <note>original: "Materia." This refers to the starting material or "First Matter" used in the alchemical process.</note> / which is required for the Stone of the Wise / is not so very costly; yet on the other hand, the understanding / the science and art are not so very poor, easy, and slight as to manufacture it. Even if the work in itself is called a "work of women and play of children" by many Philo- <note>The text cuts off mid-word. The full phrase would be "Philosophers." The phrase "work of women and play of children" (opus mulierum et ludus puerorum) is a common alchemical paradox meaning that the process is simple once the secret is understood, requiring only heat and patience like boiling a pot or playing a game.</note>

<summary>The author explains that alchemical success requires a mind focused on wisdom rather than greed, emphasizing that prayer is as essential as study. He cites Baccaser and Basil Valentine to illustrate that while the physical materials are inexpensive, the spiritual and intellectual keys are rare and difficult to obtain.</summary>

**philosophers** <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "Philosophis." Therefore, no one in this Art should rely on his sharp **intellect** <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "Ingenium." A central tenet of alchemy was that the "Great Work" could only be completed by a person of virtue with divine permission.</note>.

Indeed, excellent people and **talents** <term>talents</term> <note>original: "Ingenia." However, after their imagined opinions failed them in this way, that way, and many other ways, they finally reached the point where they despised and laughed at the entire work, holding it for naught; indeed, they even cried out that the sayings of the **philosophers** <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "Philosophorum."</note> were but sweet dreams, pleasant **discourses** <term>discourses</term> <note>original: "Discursen."</note>, or rather nothing but lies. Many people of poor understanding also undertake this most noble science, such as **barbers** <note>original: "Balbierer." fail; they therefore abandon their calling, trade, and handicraft, and begin to **distill** <term>distill</term>, **filter** <term>filter</term>, **purify** <term>purify</term>, **calcine** <term>calcine</term> <note>To heat a substance to high temperatures in order to reduce it to a powder or ash.</note>, **dissolve** <term>dissolve</term>, **coagulate** <term>coagulate</term> <note>To turn a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state.</note>, seethe, roast, and cook for so long until finally all their belongings and property are boiled away, distilled away, and spent, so that nothing more remains. Thereupon they betake themselves to other wealthy people and boast excellently of their science in **alchemy** <term>Alchymia</term>, and make many words concerning the **Theophrastic Art** <term>Theophrastic Art</term> <note>Referring to the alchemical and medical system of Paracelsus, whose birth name was Theophrastus von Hohenheim.</note> (as they call it). They claim that they have established a certain foundation for it from books and manifold experience; [they say] if only there were someone who would lend them a helping hand and advance so much that the work could be properly completed, it would be repaid to that person a hundredfold, even a thousandfold.

There are then easily found those who allow themselves to be taken in by the **deceivers'** <note>original: "Betrieger."</note> great words and final assurances (as the hearts of all men are inclined toward status and greed). They venture a good portion of their goods upon it. These patrons are then given strong hope from one month to the next, and from one year to the next, that the work will soon reach the desired end—especially when the **colors** named by the **philosophers** <term>Philosophis</term> <note>The "Philosophers" are the ancient alchemical authorities. In alchemical theory, the matter in the flask was expected to change through a specific sequence of colors—black, white, yellow, and red—as it transformed into the Philosopher's Stone.</note> reveal themselves. Then the rejoicing and exultation begins: one intends to found a church, a hospital, or an almshouse; another thinks of building castles; a third intends to recruit a host of soldiers for hire and [to help] this or

<summary>This page describes the downfall of amateur alchemists who exhaust their own wealth before turning into con artists who manipulate wealthy patrons. It details the psychological lure of alchemy, where the appearance of specific chemical colors in the laboratory is used to fuel grand fantasies of philanthropy, construction, and military power.</summary>

to bring to that potentate <term>potentate</term> <note>A monarch or ruler with great power.</note>, and thus to achieve great prestige, and to make an immortal name for himself. But it happens to them as it did to those fellows who sold the bear's skin <note>A German idiom similar to "counting one's chickens before they hatch"—selling the hide before the bear is even caught.</note> before the animal was even caught. For in the end, one sees of what tone the song is <note>original: *cujus toni*</note>, and finally it all ends on a "La-Mi" <note>original: *La Mi*. In the musical theory of the time, this refers to a specific transition between notes, but here it is used as a pun to suggest a pitiful or failing conclusion.</note>. From this it follows that those who have given credence to these **sophists** <term>sophists</term> <note>In this context, deceptive or false teachers who use clever but fallacious arguments to trick others.</note> suffer a severe blow not only to their property and livelihood but also to their good name.

Therefore, I have been moved to compassion, and Christian duty requires me anyway to warn the simple and gullible against such malicious deceivers and subtle **sophists**, if only they will allow themselves to be warned.

For it is indeed beyond all doubt that anyone who knows how to produce something so good and profitable would have no need to sell such knowledge to others for money.

Therefore, those who place their trust in such vagrants and deceivers act very foolishly; it would be better if they did as **Pope Leo X** <note>Giovanni de' Medici (1475–1521), who served as Pope from 1513 until his death.</note> did. For when a book was presented to him by a supposed gold-maker <term>gold-maker</term> <note>An alchemist claiming the secret of transmutation.</note>, which contained instructions on how one could make gold from all sorts of metals—and the man hoped to receive a stately reward <note>original: *Verehrung*, referring to a formal gift or honorarium given to an author.</note> for it—the Pope had nothing more given to him than an empty purse <note>original: *ledigen Beutel*</note>, and said:

to him: "If you can make gold, then you are richer than I, and have no need of my gold; just keep your own in this purse."

Otherwise, the true philosophers write of their highly blessed science <note>The author refers to alchemy not merely as a craft, but as a "blessed science" with a moral component.</note> that it either finds a pious, God-fearing person, or it makes an ungodly person pious and God-fearing. Now, let one observe the sophists and see what kind of life they lead; for they are to be found sooner and more often with wine, beer, tobacco, and brandy, and in other inappropriate places, than with the <term>Holy Bible</term> <note>original: *H. Bibel*</note>, the prayer book, or the beautiful books of the ancient sages. Therefore, just as the ancient sages were originally called *Sophi* <note>original: *Sophi*, meaning "wise men."</note>, and later *Philosophi* <note>original: *Philosophi*, meaning "lovers of wisdom."</note>: so too a deceiver and seller of <term>processes</term> <note>In alchemy, a "process" is a specific recipe or set of instructions for a chemical transformation.</note> may not unfairly be called a sophist and a *philoso-drunkard* <note>original: *Philosauffauß*. This is a satirical pun combining "philosopher" with the German word *saufen*, meaning to drink excessively or "booze."</note>.

But someone here might say and ask: how do I know such things that I have now written about the sophists? To him I give the friendly answer that in my youth I associated with them, sought their company, and for that reason sometimes spent a bit on them, just to win their friendship and to see what kind of furnaces, instruments <note>original: *instrumenta*</note>, and vessels they used, and with what matter <note>original: *materia*</note> they worked; there I saw and experienced many strange and foolish things, which I cannot neglect to tell the reader about.

Once, three of them made a contract <note>original: *Contract*</note> together, in which they swore such cruel and terrible oaths <note>original: *Eyd*</note> that anyone who heard the oath read aloud would have their hair stand on end from fear.

I learned during their lifetimes that they had established a contract with one another; however, the terrible oath they had attached to it—stating that they would reveal it to no person, but keep everything secret and silent—I only learned of after the death of the first man. He did not live long thereafter, and the other two also died within a few years, one after the other.

But the one who was the principal partner and who provided the funding <note>original: *Sumptus*, meaning the expenses or costs for the work.</note> could not be present during the labor, as his business dealings would not allow it. However, because he had a large income and was a great lover of alchemy <note>original: *Alchymiæ*</note>, he thought little of supporting the other two who performed the manual work.

One among these three was a scholar <note>original: *Gelährter*</note> who had a servant. I became acquainted with this servant and learned from him secretly everything the others intended and what they were dealing with.

Once, they put in a substance <note>original: *materiam*</note> that had to remain continuously <note>original: *continuè*</note> in an open fire before the bellows for three days and nights. Two of them were present to watch over it; when one went to sleep, the other would get up, and they maintained this continuously for three full days and nights. However, they could not obtain any crucibles <note>original: *Tiegel*</note> that [could withstand] in the fire...

They also intended to leave the place where they were and settle in a large, famous city in Germany <note>original: *Teutschland*</note>, rent a large house, and produce the philosopher’s stone <note>original: *lapidem phil.*</note> there in all silence. Nevertheless, he finished the rest according to the process, refined it, and separated it, but obtained nothing. He was neither moved nor angered by this, but instead believed that the art had not been properly observed—that a mistake had been made, the fire had been too strong, and that one must do it correctly. <meta>This page continues the cautionary tale from page 22 regarding a citizen who, despite his natural talents, fell into ruin by attempting to uncover the secrets of alchemy and perpetual motion through the works of Sendivogius. From this it is to be seen that this most worthy **science** <note>original: "Wissenschaft" — here referring specifically to the sacred art of alchemy</note> will not allow itself to be found and mastered by just anyone. Therefore, it is more advisable that if one is not called to it through a special inspiration from God, he should either abstain from it entirely or, after a few trials that fail, desist from it in good time.

Because I have now seen, heard, and experienced that so many persons of both high and low status have been led astray <note>original: "hinters Liecht geführet" — literally "led behind the light," an idiom for being hoodwinked</note> and shamefully betrayed by these **sophists**, and that thereby this most noble art—whose power and virtue cannot be sufficiently praised with words—has been brought into contempt; I have been moved by the impulse of God and the love I owe my neighbor to write this preface against the **sophists**, and to warn the simple sheep against the cunning wolves in sheep’s clothing.

One should diligently note what **King Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 815), a legendary figure in alchemy whose writings formed the basis of medieval European chemical knowledge</note> wrote in his second book, *The Summary of the Great and Perfect Mastery* <note>original Latin: "Summæ magni & perfecti Magisterij"</note>, chapter 30, regarding the sons of **wisdom** and **roguery** <note>original: "Weißheit und Schalckheit"</note>; his words speak thus: "Therefore, you sons of wisdom, seek..."

**Thomas Aquinas.** <note>original: "Thomas de Aquino"</note> Dear brother, by your earnest pleading you have overcome me, [persuading me] to share something understandable regarding our **Art** <term>Art: alchemy was frequently called the "Sacred Art" or "Royal Art," combining chemical experimentation with spiritual philosophy</term> and great science; and I earnestly pray you, that you would guard your mouth and not be a chatterer of these things.

**Arnold of Villanova, *The Rosary*, Book 2, Chapter 32.** <note>original: "Arnoldus Rosar. l. 2. cap. 32." — referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, a famous 14th-century alchemical treatise</note> But you, into whose hands this book comes, shall faithfully preserve the same and reveal it to no one, much less let it fall into the hands of the godless; for it perfectly contains within itself the secret of all secrets of the philosophers, and it is not fitting that such a noble pearl be cast before swine and the unworthy, since it is an exceedingly great gift of **GOD**, which **GOD** alone gives, and withdraws from whom He will.

Therefore, such people employ only futile effort and expense to track down and seek out true philosophers and to learn the **Art** from them. For he who knows something good and right will certainly not tell them, and from him who knows nothing, they can likewise learn nothing.

The only benefit they can have from it is that among those who seek this holy science and art, they get to see many a beautiful philosophical book, **hieroglyphic figures**, and various **furnaces** <term>furnaces: specifically alchemical ovens like the "Athanor," designed to maintain steady heat for months at a time</term>.

### The Sophic Hydrolith
<note>original: "Wasserstein der Weisen" (Water-Stone of the Sages). A famous alchemical treatise, often titled *Wasserstein der Weisen*, which presents the Philosophers' Stone in a Christian, spiritual context.</note>

You should also entertain such thoughts within yourself, and consider them well, that on your life—indeed, on pain of losing your salvation and bliss—you must not reveal such a secret to any unworthy or godless person, much less share it or let them enjoy it; in summary, you should not misuse it, but apply it solely to **God’s** honor, and not at all to your own fame.

**Johan de Padua.** <note>An Italian alchemist, possibly Giovanni da Padova, whose writings often emphasize the extreme secrecy required to avoid political persecution.</note> You should also hide it, as I have kept it hidden, and by no means reveal it indiscreetly, so that it may not come into the hands of the powerful and harsh men, lest you accumulate eternal damnation. **The same author:** It is not **God's** will to share this high treasure with high, powerful lords and **potentates** <term>potentates <note>Monarchs or rulers with great power. Alchemists feared that if a tyrant possessed the secret of making gold, it would lead to war and economic ruin.</note></term>.

**Brother Basil Valentine in the Twelfth Key**
<note>Basil Valentine was a legendary alchemist and monk. His "Twelve Keys" are a series of symbolic steps describing the preparation of the Philosophers' Stone.</note>

For whoever would do so would be cursed, would bring the wrath of God upon himself, and would die of a stroke <note>original: "am Schlage sterben." This implies a sudden, divine punishment or a fatal "blow" from heaven.</note>.

**Brother Basil Valentine in *The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony*:** <note>original: "Triumphwagen des Antimonij." Basil Valentine was a legendary 15th-century Benedictine monk and alchemist; this specific book is one of the most famous texts on the medicinal uses of the metal antimony.</note> For this is indeed certain, and I know it to be true, that no godless person shall obtain the true medicine, much less taste the right, unchanging, sweet bread of heaven of eternity.

**Aristotle in *The Secret of Secrets*** <note>original: "Secretis Secretorum." A medieval treatise traditionally attributed to Aristotle, containing advice to Alexander the Great on various matters including alchemy and statecraft.</note> says that he who reveals the secrets of Nature and Art is a breaker of the heavenly seal.

The reason why I now introduce so many **arguments**, <note>original: "argumenta"</note> demonstrate, and prove that all true philosophers have attached a curse to all those who intend to **divulge** <note>original: "divulgiren"</note> and make public this holy science, is so that the **magnates** <term>magnates <note>People of high rank, wealth, or power, such as princes or dukes.</note></term> may no longer allow themselves to be fooled by **sophists** <term>sophists <note>In alchemy, "sophists" or "souffleurs" were fraudulent practitioners who used deceptive tricks to appear successful, as opposed to the "true" philosophers.</note></term> and cheaters, and so that the simple and the fools might become wiser, and that the "bloodhounds" <note>An idiom for those who hunt for secrets or wealth with aggressive persistence.</note> should stop tracking this hidden science. For no philosopher is permitted to give away this jewel or golden crown with a purple hood to other unworthy people, nor to crown them with it; for they have left such things to God’s discretion, that He may reveal it to whom He wills, as can be read further in **Nicolas Flamel**, chapter 2. <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a French scribe who became legendary as an alchemist who allegedly discovered the Philosophers' Stone.</note>

Because I am also aware that not all disciples

The second booklet was written by a learned monk experienced in the Art named **Antonius de Abbatia**, who lived roughly after the death of **Lull** <note>original: "Lullii." Referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a major Catalan philosopher and mystic to whom many alchemical works were attributed.</note>. In alchemy, "labor" specifically refers to the physical work performed at the furnace or with laboratory apparatus.</note></term>. Finally, he reached his goal through constant prayer, **meditation** <note>original: "meditiren." In this context, meditation is a focused mental and spiritual discipline used to understand the hidden mysteries of nature.</note>, and labor, attaining the desired end of his search. He left behind the information contained in his book—the kind which can only be found in the **writings of the true philosophers** <note>original: "wahren philoſophen ſcriptis"</note>—to his **fellow monks** <note>original: "Conventualibus," referring to his brothers within the monastic community.</note>...

Regarding the difficulty of the Art, he himself writes thus: It does not, however, happen so easily / that one arrives at the unchangeable knowledge of these three main points through his own diligence and understanding / and one can stumble in an instant: He must receive oral instruction from a faithful master / otherwise things go quite badly / for it requires a lengthy experience / together with a great knowledge of all natural creatures: for how many are there / who enter into this art / and yet spend many years / before they can find the right *matter* or *subject of the Tincture* <note>original: "materiam oder Subjectum Tincturæ." A maze or path of error, suggesting that a literal reading of alchemical texts often leads to failure.</note>.

## To the Most Honorable / Highly Esteemed and Very Noble
# Master EDITOR
### Of these three extraordinary **chemical writings** <term>chemical writings: in the 17th century, "chemical" (chymisch) often referred specifically to alchemical works concerned with transmutation and the Philosopher's Stone.</term>.

Much worthy Sir and Friend! I have recently heard /
  That it was not without great effort that you came to possess /
  Three old writings / which have never before been in print /
  Composed regarding the Art <note>The "Art" (Kunst) refers to the Great Work of alchemy.</note> in the very best way.
You have with certain toil gathered these three together /
They have never before been under the press nor in the German tongue /
  You have not / as you could have / desired them for yourself alone /
  But wanted / that through them / whoever wished / might be taught.
From Bohemia, Hungary, and from England you bring them /
To us in the German Empire / in which you have well succeeded /
  You make it so that they speak to us Germans in German /
  So that whoever is a German / may well understand them.
Whoever is **skilled in the art** <note>original: "Kunst-verständig." This refers to someone possessing the deep, technical knowledge required to understand alchemical allegories.</note> / sees who they have been /
Who possessed, knew, taught, and read them ;
  Whoever is experienced in the Art / looks upon their writings /
  As something from which juice and strength <note>A metaphor for the vital essence, efficacy, or "marrow" of the alchemical secrets.</note> can be richly taken ;
Who can thank you enough / that you have published [them] /
Which have never been seen in the German tongue in human memory /
  The **Frangmann** <note>original: "Frangmann." ## To the Honorable, Most Highly
## Esteemed and Right Noble

### Writings.

Much valued Sir and Friend! I have recently
->learned,<-

That not without effort there have come into your hands

Composed concerning the Art <term>Art: The "Great Art" (Ars Magna), a traditional term for alchemy and the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone.</term> in the very best manner.

Those who are just beginning, you warn against the Sophists <note>In an alchemical context, "Sophists" refers to "puffers" or fraudulent practitioners who used deceptive rhetoric and false chemical tricks rather than true natural philosophy.</note>,
Against those frivolous, loose fellows, those true "all-mixers," <note>original: "Allgemisten." A derogatory pun on "Alchemists," suggesting they are people who merely mix random substances together (all-mix) without scientific or spiritual understanding.</note>
Recounting how wickedly this rabble has behaved,
Now here, now elsewhere, in this or that city.
You show the right path, the way to attain the Art <term>Art: The "Great Art" (Ars Magna), the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone and the transmutation of base metals into gold.</term>,
The very path that you yourself have taken from the Sages,
The path to the right purpose, the path to the single goal;
Many thousands do not know it, and yet so many seek it.
You teach that it is the gift of the Great God,
That one receives it only from His grace and nothing else,
When one prays devoutly, and meditates upon these matters,
And then works diligently upon it: so that one may possess it.
Those devoted to the Art, who have experienced much therein,
Who have spent much time, many days and years upon it,
They shall thank you, once they have been taught
Through these writings, which have now been made public by you.
Fame <note>original: "Die Fam'." Referring to Fama, the personification of rumor and renown, often depicted as a winged figure with a trumpet.</note> shall spread your praise and glory as far
As the bright radiance of the sun bestrides the earth;
Fame shall cry out, and make widely known
Your piety and integrity through every city and land.

->To his much-valued Lord and Friend<-
->this is dedicated out of a service-oriented spirit by<-
->**Arnoldus Körner, Doctor**<-
->Student of the Chemical Art.<-

## Dialogue of the Alchemist and Alchemy.

->The Alchemist.<-

**B**ehold, O Alchemy, Princess rich in gifts,
Of whom for a long time we have heard nothing,
Blessed is the day on which I see you here,
In such a beautiful dress, O most beloved ornament!
Where have you been hiding, that in so many years
No one has experienced anything of your virtuous power?
Is there then no one left, O great Queen,
Who may see you clearly and openly with their eyes?

(41.)

## Alchemy. <note>original: "Alchimia." In this era, Alchemy was frequently personified as a noble woman, a goddess, or a queen who reveals secrets only to the worthy.</note>

Oh, my dear friend / why do you lament so,
That you have heard nothing of me for so long?
I do indeed travel through the world / visiting many a city and land /
Though my great power is still known to few.
For a time, I was indeed within my castle,
Locked away and guarded. Though many have read of it—
Both the learned and the unlearned / on how to enter therein /
Yet they were driven away by the darkness. <note>This "darkness" refers to the "Hermetic" style of writing—intentionally obscure metaphors meant to hide secrets from the "unworthy."</note>
Many a proud spirit thought to capture me with cunning,
But when he saw / that he could not succeed /
He did not hesitate to wound my honor,
As if I were never born legitimate and true. <note>Alchemy often had to defend its reputation against critics who claimed it was a "bastard" science or a fraud.</note>
Many a Sophist <term>Sophist: In alchemy, a "sophist" is a fraud or a "puffer"—someone who uses fake chemical tricks or deceptive language to cheat people out of money.</term> has also dared,
Though he had never once set eyes upon me,
To claim / as long as he was paid in cold cash /
That he could clearly show the certain way to find me.
Now, a wise man / who wishes me very well /
And has drawn the true meaning from the writings of the Sages,
Has dressed me anew in this well-made garment,
So that everyone can read of me in the German language. <note>original: "Teutscher Sprach." Before this period, most alchemical secrets were kept in Latin; translating them into the "vernacular" (common German) was a bold step toward making the "Art" accessible.</note>
He has also clearly described the Sophists' tricks for you;
Pray, read these as a warning /
And follow the right path / which is shown here /
So you may / if God wills / attain my adornment. <note>The "adornment" or "ornament" refers to the ultimate prize of alchemy: the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

## The Alchemist. <note>original: "Alchimista."</note>

O Goddess of great power / to you, rightful thanks are due,
And also to the wise master / who has so adorned you /
With such a bright garment; now you emerge from the contempt
Into which the crowd of Sophists had shamefully
brought you.
Now, Master, continue to write of such things,
Which make the path to the noble Art <term>The Art: Short for the "Hermetic Art" or "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), the process of refining base metals into gold and the soul into perfection.</term> smooth for us,
So shall your praise and fame endure,
As long as the River Elbe flows down to the sea. <note>original: "Elbe-Strohm." The mention of the Elbe suggests the author or printer lived in a region like Saxony, Bohemia, or near Hamburg.</note>

->Chrisenius Rhodobessus.<- <note>This is likely a pseudonym (an assumed name) for the author. "Chrisenius" is derived from the Greek word for gold (*chrysos*). Many alchemists hid their true identities to avoid persecution or unwanted attention.</note>

### John of Tetzen
# THE PROCEDURE
### concerning the Stone of the Philoso-
### phers,

Those seeking the secret of the art <note>original: "Occultum artis inquirentes." This refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>
Should first say as follows,
->In the name of Jesus Christ, Amen.<-
He who is salvation, fountain, and life,
In whom the supported art shines forth
->By the protection of grace.<- <note>Alchemists believed that success was a *Donum Dei* (Gift of God), granted only through divine favor.</note>
He bestows it upon whom He wishes,
And by withdrawing it, takes away
->The mystery with its veil.<- <note>original: "Problema cum velamine." This refers to the "Hermetic" concealment of secrets; if God does not grant the light of understanding, the text remains a mystery.</note>
Therefore, let the worker pray
To the Creator, who is watchful
->In the gathering of this art.<-
O Jesus, hope of the wretched,
And revealer to the fallen,
->Illuminate my mind with a beam<-

This paper of truth
->To be composed in this study.<- <note>The author is asking for divine guidance as he begins to write down the chemical process.</note>
Grant me [deliverance from] a corrupt life,
So that after death I may
->Deserve to enter, by your help,<-
Into the holy, joyful host
Of heavenly Angels
->With sweet songs of praise.<-

## Johannis von Tetzen
# THE PROCESS
## Concerning the Stone of the Philosophers.

You students of this great art, <note>The "great art" refers to alchemy, specifically the *Ars Magna* or the quest for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>
Begin with God’s grace and favor
->In the Name of Jesus Christ.<-
He is the life, salvation, and light;
In Him place your confidence:
->He holds the art in His hands.<- <note>A common alchemical theme: the secrets of nature are a divine gift (*donum Dei*) and cannot be attained by intellect alone.</note>
He gives and takes it from whom He wills;
Set no measure or limit for Him.
->Without Him, nothing can be accomplished.<-
Therefore lift up your heart to **GOD**;
He can help you in times of need;
->Ask Him for His grace.<-
O **JESUS**! You are my hope,
And You help at every moment
->The poor and the wretched.<-
Grant me now the Holy Spirit,
That He may be my help and support,
->In completing this little book.<- <note>The author seeks divine inspiration not just for the chemical work, but for the act of writing down the instructions.</note>
Grant also that after this time,
Freed from sins through Your office,
->I may enter the Kingdom of Heaven.<-
To laud and praise Your name,
With all the dear little angels,
->May I be received.<-

<term>John of Tetzen</term> (Johannis von Tetzen): A 15th-century figure often associated with alchemical writings that blend laboratory instructions with deep Christian piety.
<term>Alchemy</term>: The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter and the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone.
<term>Philosopher's Stone</term>: A legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the Elixir of Life.
<term>Process</term>: The specific sequence of laboratory operations (the "recipe") required to create the Stone.
<term>Christian Alchemy</term>: A tradition that views the purification of metals as a parallel to the spiritual purification of the human soul.

<summary>This page serves as a devotional prologue to the alchemical instructions, presenting a rhyming prayer that emphasizes that success in the "Great Work" depends entirely on the grace of Jesus Christ. It establishes the author's intent to record the process as a divinely sanctioned task meant for the benefit of the "poor and wretched."</summary>

Though triple in Person,
It is yet one thing in its gifts,
-> Unmoved in its essence. <- <note>The author equates the three alchemical principles—Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury—with the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. They are distinct in function but unified in the final "Stone."</note>

The Father and the Son stand firm,
So that the Water rises at its best,
-> Falling down drop by drop. <- <note>A description of distillation: the substance is heated until it rises as vapor (the "Son" rising) and then cools to fall back as liquid (the "rain").</note>

Then the Earth is made wet;
The three are then brought into one,
-> Firmly linked together again. <-

By the Father’s breath, the Son climbs high
From below, and reaches the heavens,
-> Guided so high by the Spirit. <- <note>The "Spirit" here refers to the volatile essence or gas that carries the matter upward during the heating process.</note>

When this and the former are bound,
Tied into one through steadfast love,
-> Prepared as is proper. <-

The body is made clear and bright
When the soul is brought back to it,
-> And given into it once more. <- <note>In the final stages of the work, the "soul" (the purified volatile essence) is reunited with the "body" (the refined solid matter), a process known as "redemption" or "fixation."</note>

It shall then live for all eternity;
It shall be ready to be immortal,
-> To possess eternal life. <- <note>The result is the Philosopher’s Stone, which was believed to be a "fixed" and indestructible substance capable of perfecting both metals and the human body.</note>

<summary>This page presents a German poem describing the chemical and spiritual stages of creating the Philosopher's Stone, using the Holy Trinity as a metaphor for the union of the three alchemical principles. It details the process of distillation and the eventual "glorification" of the material body when it is reunited with its soul.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Holy Trinity, elements, distillation, Philosopher's Stone, spiritual transformation, German poetry, salt, sulphur, mercury</keywords>

# Johannes de Tetzen’s Process.

To whom the Father expressed this
And impressed it for the servants of God
->Through the saturation of the parchment.<- <note>original: "Per Chartæ imbuentiam." This likely refers to the transmission of secret knowledge through the written word or the "soaking" of the material with spiritual truth.</note>

You who desire the gift of God,
Contemplate of this matter
->The flowing (and most lovely) fountain.<-

To transmute the Moon and the Sun,
I command you with a clear voice:
->The indissoluble (dripping) water.<- <note>original: "Lunam, Solem... Lympham instillabilem (insolubilem)." In the coded language of alchemy, the "Moon" is Silver and the "Sun" is Gold. The "water" is the philosophical mercury used to dissolve them.</note>

This is the rare first root,
Given to us, and held most dear,
->By the testimony of Hermes.<- <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy and author of the Emerald Tablet.</note>

He truly reports from the Eternal One
That he holds this fountain of the Art
->With the discourse of the scripture.<-

Here I now hand over to you the egg,
In which you may take a new song
->Of the treatment of our stone.<- <note>The "egg" (original: "ovum") refers to the *philosophical egg*, the oval glass vessel in which the alchemical materials are heated and transformed into the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

Above gold and silver
And precious ointment,
->This is preferred, even over jasper.<-

To the goodness of little gems
And of herbs, I am the tincture;
->By the strength of salvation:<- <note>A <term>tincture</term> is a concentrated substance believed to have the power to "stain" or transform the very nature of a base metal into gold, or to heal a diseased body.</note>

It cures the diseases of the weary
And preserves the power of the healthy
->In the wellness of their limbs.<-

It is named the Elixir of Life,
In which the poisonous sting
->Of death is driven away.<-

<summary>A Latin alchemical poem by Johannes de Tetzen describing the divine gift of the "Philosophical Egg" and the medicinal powers of the Elixir of Life to cure disease and transmute metals.</summary>

<meta>This page provides a German rhyming version of the Latin hymn found on page 60. It continues the instructions of Johannes de Tetzen, moving from the theoretical origins of the "Art" to the specific metaphors of the alchemical "Egg" and the medicinal "Elixir."</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>

# Johannes de Tetzen’s Process.

To whom the Father has commanded
To show the Art to God’s servant
In writing, as indicated before.
If you desire for yourself God’s gift,
Then let your mind be directed
Toward this matter’s little fountain.
Gold and silver you shall transform,
As I teach you here,
Into a flowing little water. <note>In alchemical symbolism, "flowing water" refers to the "mercurial" state where metals are dissolved into their primary essence.</note>
This is the beginning, this the foundation,
Which Hermes has granted us,
Who teaches the Art in this way. <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>
He who knows the fountain may well say
That the matter has come to him from God
To know the Art upon the earth.
Take the egg that I give you,
Hear my teaching and follow me,
In how the Stone is to be made. <note>The <term>Philosophical Egg</term> is the name for the oval glass vessel, or 'aludel,' in which the alchemical materials are sealed and heated.</note>
Gold, silver, and the like,
And whatever is found precious on the earth,
Must yield to these things.
All gemstones and herbal juices,
All resin, wood, and root sap,
Are to be found within it.
All who use it are healthy;
It drives away sickness in an instant,
And can overcome all things.
It is called an Elixir
Of Life; as is well known,
It strongly resists death.

<summary>This page presents a German verse rendition of the alchemical process, identifying the "Philosophical Egg" as the vessel of transformation and describing the resulting Elixir as a universal medicine superior to all earthly riches.</summary>

What the master teacher <note>original: *imbutoris*, literally one who imbues or dyes. Alchemy was often called the "art of dyeing."</note> has produced
Is a sweet fruit; of flowery scent
->Is this little apple.<-

Sublime Aristotle,
With his divine sayings of the Art,
->Hiding the secret under a veil.<- <note>In the Middle Ages, many alchemical texts were falsely attributed to Aristotle to give them authority.</note>

He composes this matter in words,
And sounds it out with a living voice,
->In his own discourse.<-

[He says] it shall be one, and not many,
Which thieves cannot
->Root out by plundering.<- <note>The "Art" is a spiritual and intellectual treasure that cannot be physically stolen.</note>

Cook the egg in heat as well,
Only in the manner of the Sun, <note>This refers to a gentle, consistent heat, similar to the sun warming the earth, rather than a violent flame.</note>
->With gentle management.<-

Beware that the egg is not broken,
Nor destroyed by a touch,
->Within the Solar circle.<- <note>The "Solar circle" refers to the duration of the heating process or the specialized furnace used by the alchemist.</note>

Until the color is held fast,
The egg shall also be guided
->Into the brightness of whiteness.<- <note>The <term>Albedo</term> (Whiteness) is the second major stage of the alchemical work, signifying purification.</note>

Know that the yolk lies hidden
Within this egg; it wishes to redden <note>The "yolk" represents the hidden "Gold" or "Sulfur" within the matter.</note>
->With the splendor of redness.<- <note>The <term>Rubedo</term> (Redness) is the final stage of the process, signifying the completion of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

Our river is pleasant,
By which, while handling the King of Kings, <note>The "King of Kings" is a metaphor for the most noble substance, often gold or the Stone itself.</note>
->You shall wash him eagerly.<-

Let the King be clothed in a double garment,
And let our King be decorated
->Sweetly with a little rose.<- <note>The "double garment" refers to the union of spirit and soul. The rose is a common symbol for the perfected Stone.</note>

A sweet fruit, a beautiful apple,
Of lovely scent from a high throne,
Can prolong life for a long time.
The wise Greek <note>original: *Griech*. This refers to Aristotle, to whom many alchemical works were falsely attributed in the Middle Ages.</note>, highly renowned,

Has also taught in a hidden way.

That there be one Stone, and no more,
Which no thief—however evil or however much
He may wish—can steal from you.
Cook this egg in the heat of the sun,
Cook it very moderately, so that it sweats
Quite gently; you will not fail.
See that the egg does not break;
When you handle it, do not be too bold,
Just let it stand in silence,

Our river is very beautiful and bright,
In which the King must bathe,
So that he becomes white and pure. <note>In alchemy, the "King" represents the metallic soul or gold, and his "bath" refers to the process of dissolution and purification.</note>
The King shall have two garments,
With which he shall be clothed,
Well adorned with red.

->In which he may triumph.<- <note>The "white garment" represents the <term>Albedo</term> (whiteness), a stage of purification. The "purple" or "red" robe represents <term>Rubedo</term> (redness), the final perfection of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

->Yet quite lovely all at once.<- <note>The "tailor" is a metaphor for the heat of the furnace, which "sews" or fixes the colors onto the material.</note>

With four threads, so that it may
->Appear like the glow of the dawn.<- <note>The "four threads" represent the four classical elements (Earth, Air, Fire, and Water) being brought into perfect balance. The "dawn" (Aurora) is a common symbol for the beginning of the final successful stage.</note>

->Let this be said to the pious.<-

->To him who has received it.<-
It is given only to those

->And ordained by God.<-

<meta>This page continues the alchemical poem from page 65. <page-num>(66.)</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

They shine with a radiant spirit,
Singing to the gracious God
->A jubilant symphony.<- <note>In alchemical literature, the successful completion of a stage is often accompanied by prayers of thanksgiving and "music of the spheres," signaling harmony between the chemist and the divine.</note>

Who for a small deed grants the good,
In this life and in the heavens,
->A truly hundredfold reward.<- <note>original: *centuplum*. This reflects the alchemical promise that a small amount of the "Seed" or Stone can transform a vast quantity of base metal, or a small amount of spiritual effort leads to great grace.</note>

Let our **Stone** be dissolved,
Let its water be given to it,
->As much as is necessary.<- <note>original: *Lapis*. This refers to the <term>Solutio</term> stage, where the solid matter is broken down into a liquid state to be purified.</note>

Let such a degree [of heat] be maintained,
That the whole stone is submerged,
->Through various regimens.<- <note>A "regimen" is a specific period of the process governed by a particular planetary influence or heat level.</note>

When the **white sulfur** is moistened,
The "water of milk" is distilled
->With a flowing motion.<- <note>original: *aqua lactis*. "Milk water" or "Virgin's Milk" is a symbolic term for the white, mercurial liquid that nourishes the substance during the <term>Albedo</term> (white) stage.</note>

Our lemon-colored sulfur,
Which turns red like wine,
->In a splendid color.<- <note>original: *concitrinum*. This refers to the <term>Citrinitas</term> stage, a yellowing that historically occurred between the white and red stages of the Great Work.</note>

For this is the power in which is revealed—
And where all strength lies hidden—
->The Mastery of the Art.<- <note>original: *Artis magisterium*. The "Mastery" is the secret knowledge required to successfully project the Stone and achieve transmutation.</note>

By which the one who knows is exalted,
And being lifted up, is refreshed
->Through sweet relief.<-

The first three [stages] of the skilled
Masters are thus concluded
->In distinct months.<-

The following is handled for two [months],
The third is adorned by the sun
->In certain quantities.<- <note>The alchemist tracks the progress of the work by the calendar; "adorned by the sun" refers to the final <term>Rubedo</term> (red stage), which is associated with the Sun and Gold.</note>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical poem from page 66. They thank Him for great understanding,
When they, with hands uplifted,
->Stand praising before Him.<- <note>In alchemical philosophy, "understanding" or *Verstand* is considered a divine gift required to interpret the cryptic allegories of the Art.</note>

To the pious, for a little, He gives much,
Here the Lord, without measure or limit,
->Indeed, a hundredfold in return.<- <note>original: *hundertfältig*. This refers to the concept of "multiplication," where the finished Stone can transmute vastly larger quantities of base metal into gold.</note>

Very often you must soak our **Stone**,
Pour out for it so much of its water
->As much as it may desire.<- <note>This describes <term>Imbibition</term>, a process where the dry "Stone" is repeatedly saturated with its own distilled "mercurial water" to nourish it, much like a plant.</note>

So that the **Stone** becomes wet through and through,
->By the Art and wise teachings.<-

Dissolve the **white sulfur**, and so
A water is distilled like milk,
->Which shall be very fluid.<- <note>This refers to the "Virgin's Milk," a white liquid that appears during the <term>Albedo</term> stage, signifying purification.</note>

Dissolve the sulfur, yellow and pure,
You shall see it like a red wine,
->Shining transparently in the glass.<- <note>This marks the transition through <term>Citrinitas</term> (yellowing) toward the <term>Rubedo</term> (red stage), which is the final perfection of the Stone.</note>

Herein lies the whole power
And strength of the **Art**; consider this well,
->So it comes to a good end.<- <note>original: *Kunst*. In this context, "the Art" refers specifically to Alchemy (*Alchymia*).</note>

He who is wise, clever, and highly learned
Is held in great esteem and honored,
->Remaining free from all misery.<-

The first stage that occurs in the **Art**
Is carried out by the wise
->In three times thirty days:<- <note>90 days is a traditional period for the first major regimen, often associated with the slow decay of the matter (nigredo).</note>

In two moons the second is completed,
The third is adorned by the **Sun**,
->According to a certain measure, they say:<- <note>The "two moons" (60 days) likely refers to the Albedo, while the "Sun" represents the final red stage or gold-making powder.</note>

<meta>This page continues the alchemical poem from page 67, moving from the description of the Albedo (white) and Citrinitas (yellow) stages into the final technical instructions for the Stone’s "multiplication" and "congelation." It emphasizes the moral requirements of the practitioner and provides specific warnings about heat management.</meta>

When you follow these steps <note>original Latin: *Hos cum gradus prosequaris*</note>
You will be loved by the citizens of the world
->Out of the abundance of things.<- <note>Successful alchemy was believed to grant the practitioner immense wealth and social standing, though the "citizens of the world" are often contrasted with the "sons of art" who seek spiritual truth.</note>

Through one thing further you shall attain
This power, and do not wonder at it,
->In its multiplication.<- <note><term>Multiplication</term> is the stage where the finished Stone’s power is increased so that a tiny amount can transmute a vast quantity of base metal into gold.</note>

So may you live in the praise of Christ,
You who seek, without deceit,
->The excellence of the Art.<-

You have everything; thus, in your whole
Devoted heart, keep it safe
->Through the mercy of God.<-

It remains now only to declare
What it is to congeal the gem,
->To form the Stone into a shape.<- <note><term>Congelation</term> is the process of turning the liquid "mercurial" matter into a solid, stable physical form.</note>

To make the **Stone** evaporate,
Be sure to dry it with fire
->According to the rule of governance.<- <note>The <term>Regimen</term> refers to the careful management of the furnace’s heat over months of the "Great Work."</note>

Beware of a flood <note>original Latin: *confluxione*</note>
When you are at the fourth dissolution,
->Lest the **Stone** be corrupted.<- <note>A common warning in alchemy was to avoid adding too much solvent or "water" to the matter, which would "drown" the internal fire and ruin the experiment.</note>

The **sulfur** then will be subtle;
It also requires a slow fire,
->In which it is thus converted.<-

The more you shall disperse,
And by dissolving, shall congeal
->The essence of the **Stone**,<- <note>This refers to the cyclical process of *Solve et Coagula* (dissolve and coagulate). Every time the Stone is dissolved and then re-solidified, its potency increases.</note>

The more you shall gather together,
And by tinging, you shall color it
->Through its own increase.<- <note><term>Tinging</term> is the final act where the Stone imparts its "color" (its perfected nature) to base metals, turning them into gold or silver.</note>

It warns the practitioner against "drowning" the Stone during dissolution and emphasizes that the Stone's power grows through repeated cycles of dissolving and solidifying.</summary>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical poem from page 68, moving from the philosophical preparations to the final technical stages of the Great Work: multiplication and congelation. It emphasizes that repeated cycles of dissolving and drying the matter are necessary to perfect the Stone’s power and transparency.</meta>

(69.)

If you follow these matters rightly,
Everyone may love you
->Because of your prosperity.<- <note>In alchemical literature, material success is often promised as a secondary benefit to spiritual enlightenment, though it required the practitioner to remain "pious and honest" to maintain their standing.</note>

If you begin once more anew,
You will wonder at what it can do,
->When you see the quantity.<- <note>This refers to the <term>multiplication</term> of the Stone, where its volume or its transmutative power is exponentially increased through repeated cycles of the process.</note>

If you desire the **Art** with great diligence,
To maintain it for your praise and prize,
->Then live piously and honestly.<- <note>Alchemists believed that the secrets of the Stone would only remain with those who were morally upright; greed or malice would cause the "Art" to fail.</note>

You have the **Art** here entirely;
Keep it within your heart,
->It will not be a burden to you.<-

I must teach you one more thing now,
What it is to wisely <term>congeal</term>,
->So that the **Stone** becomes transparent.<- <note><term>Congelation</term> is the process of turning a liquid substance into a solid. A successful "Philosophers' Stone" was often described as looking like a heavy, transparent, ruby-red glass.</note>

Boil down the **Water** gradually;
Through **Fire** make your **Stone** dry.
->This should happen through the Art alone.<-

When it has been dissolved four times,
Do not ruin it; use good skill,
->Lest it flow together.<- <note>The text warns against "confluxion" or improper melting, which occurs if the heat is managed poorly during the sensitive final stages of crystallization, causing the matter to fuse into a useless mass.</note>

The **Sulphur** then will be subtle;
A gentle fire is also its due,
->In which it may well be enclosed.<- <note><term>Sulphur</term> represents the active, masculine principle and the "soul" of the matter. It must be "fixed" so it no longer evaporates when heated.</note>

The more you shall dissolve it
And bring it back together again
->So that it rightly withstands,<- <note>This "withstanding" refers to <term>fixation</term>—the ability of the Stone to remain solid and stable even under the most intense heat of a furnace.</note>

The more you increase its power,
Its coloring and clear **Juice**,

In the Name of the Lord, AMEN.

(78)

I exhort you, most beloved, in the fear of the Lord <note>original: "timore Domini"; a traditional religious framing used to emphasize the gravity and spiritual danger of the alchemical pursuit.</note>, that you strive to circumvent no one through fraud, lest you deserve a damnable and perpetual curse.

Again I admonish you, do not lie [by claiming] that you possess the gift of God <note>original: "donum Dei"; the "Philosopher's Stone" or the knowledge of its creation was frequently described as a divine gift granted only to the virtuous.</note>, since a lie should be far from you. <meta>This page continues the discourse on the "secret art" mentioned on the previous page. (80.)

[...se]cret, you must know the <term>first matter</term> <note>Latin: "primam materiam"; in alchemy, this refers to the original, formless base substance from which all metals and the Philosopher's Stone are derived.</note> of those bodies; otherwise, you will frustrate your labor. The first matter is not the **common Mercury** <note>Latin: "Mercurius vulgi"; ordinary quicksilver available to the public, as opposed to the specialized "Mercury of the Sages."</note>, but it is that of the Wise—that is, an **unctuous and moist vapor**. For from the moist, the mineral stone is made, and from the unctuous, the metallic body is made; for it is necessary that the bodies be converted into such an unctuous vapor, and by such conversion, the bodies are destroyed. And thus the grain (or gum) of the body is cast down into death and is totally **mortified** <note>Latin: "mortificatur"; the alchemical process of "killing" or breaking down a substance so it can be reborn in a higher form.</note>.

And understand it thus: Unless the grain of wheat—that is, unless the gum of the body—is first converted into its first matter, namely into an unctuous vapor (which is the Mercury of the Philosophers and the Sages), it will remain alone and without fruit <note>This is a direct alchemical application of the biblical verse John 12:24: "Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit."</note>.

And such a vapor is called the **Stone** in the well-known chapters of books, and it is the beginning of our operation, and the **unctuous sulfur**, from which, at the completion, is extracted the <term>Fifth Essence</term> <note>Latin: "Quinta Essentia" or Quintessence; the highest and purest state of matter, believed to be the medicinal and transformative soul of the substance.</note>, the **tincturing Mercury**, and every body acting against nature...

<summary>This page defines the "First Matter" of alchemy not as common quicksilver, but as a specific "unctuous vapor" derived from the symbolic death and mortification of metallic bodies. It emphasizes that without this conversion into a vaporous state, the alchemical "grain" cannot bear fruit or produce the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<keywords>First Matter, Mercury of the Sages, unctuous vapor, mortification, Quintessence, sulfur, alchemy theory, grain of wheat allegory</keywords>

(81)

...**two bodies**, the white and red **sulfur of the Sages** <note>original: "Sulphur der Weisen"; in alchemical theory, sulfur represents the active, masculine principle and the internal fire of matter.</note> is found, in which wise Nature contains her seed.

Because the tinting rays are present in these with the help of our salt, you—whoever desires to explore the secret of this art—must learn to know the **first matter** <note>original: "primam materiam"; the formless base substance of all things.</note> of these same bodies; otherwise, your labor will be in vain.

But the **first matter** is not common **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"</note>, but that of the Sages; that is, a greasy and white vapor. For from the moisture, the mineral **stone** <note>original: "lapis"</note> is formed, and from the greasy or fatty part, a metallic body is produced. For the bodies must be converted into such a greasy vapor, and through such a transformation, the bodies are also killed <note>The alchemical process of "mortification," where the physical form is destroyed to liberate the spirit.</note>.

And in this way, the **grain** <note>original: "Körnlein"</note> of the body is laid down into death and is completely deprived of life.

And you must understand it thus: "Unless the grain falls into the earth," etc., which is as much as saying: if the **gum** <note>original: "Gummi"; refers to the resinous or binding substance of the metal.</note> of the body is not first transformed into its **first matter**—that is, into a greasy vapor, namely into the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Mercurium Philosophorum"</note> and of the Sages—it will remain alone and without fruit <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 12:24, used here to describe the necessity of the "chemical death" of materials.</note>.

Now, such a vapor is called the **Stone**, which is well known in the chapters of the books, and it is the beginning of our work, and the greasy **sulfur**, from which the **fifth essence** <note>original: "Quinta Essentia"; the purest essence of a substance, beyond the four physical elements.</note> finally [arises]...

# Proof from Holy Divine Scripture,

<meta>This page continues the shift from practical alchemical instruction to the theoretical and spiritual foundations of the craft. (84.)
<header>Of the *First Matter* <note>original: "Prima Materia"; the formless, primordial substance believed to be the starting point for creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of the Philosophical Stone.</header>

### By what Names the *First Matter* has been called by the Ancient, True, and Credible Philosophers.

<margin>Hermes</margin> Two mercuries. <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy.</note> Chapter 3.
<margin>Democr.</margin> Two seeds which are not found upon the earth of the living. <note>Democritus, the ancient Greek philosopher, to whom many early alchemical works were attributed.</note>
<margin>Arteph.</margin> The only active thing in the entire world in this art. <note>Artephius, a 12th-century writer known for works on the longevity of life and the Secret Book.</note>
<margin>Geber</margin> A triple smoke. <note>original: "dreyfacher Rauch"; likely referring to the three stages of vaporization or the three alchemical principles.</note> Book 1, chapter 27. <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath and foundational figure in chemistry.</note>
<margin>Synesius</margin> The *Proper Agent* <note>original: "Proprium agens"; the specific force or substance that triggers a transformation.</note>.
<margin>Mar. pr.</margin> Two gums, also two smokes. <note>Maria Prophetissa, or Mary the Jewess, an early alchemist credited with inventing various laboratory apparatus.</note>
<margin>Flamell.</margin> The *First Agent* <note>original: "Primum Agens"</note>. Also: The actual moisture; the viscous and metallic radical moisture. Also: two dragons, one with wings, the other without wings. <note>Nicolas Flamel used the imagery of dragons to represent the volatile (winged) and fixed (unwinged) principles of matter.</note>
<margin>Bernhar.</margin> A twofold or doubled *Mercury*. <note>Bernard of Trevisan, a 15th-century Italian alchemist.</note>
<margin>Basilius</margin> A twofold *Mercury*, volatile and fixed.
<margin>Theoph.</margin> The *First Root* <note>original: "Radix prima"</note>. (Sun & Moon) <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541); the symbols ☉ and ☽ represent Gold/Sulfur and Silver/Mercury.</note>
<margin>Sendiv.</margin> Two souls, flying over a small river. <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a Polish alchemist famous for his work on "central nitre" or oxygen.</note>
<margin>Clei. Baur</margin> The threefold point of the *Universal*. <note>Cleidophorus Baur is likely a reference to a 17th-century hermetic author.</note>
<margin>J. Böh.</margin> Two *Central* fires. <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), the German mystic who used alchemical language to describe the spiritual nature of God and the soul.</note>

Without the providence and inspiration of God, no one will understand what the *First Matter* is. If God graces and gifts someone with it, then the gift of secrecy, humility, caution, and watchfulness is imparted and granted to him by God, so that he does not offer it to anyone for money, as the Sophists <note>In alchemical texts, "sophists" refers to fraudulent "puffers" or charlatans who lack true spiritual understanding and seek only to make money.</note> do; therefore it is manifest that their deception is mere lies, bragging, and fraud.

It concludes by asserting that the true nature of this substance can only be understood through divine inspiration and must be kept secret from fraudulent charlatans.</summary>

### By what Names the *First Matter* has been called by the Ancient, True, and Credible Philosophers.

<margin>Hermes</margin> Two mercuries. <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy and hermetic philosophy.</note> Chapter 3.

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory address by Antonius de Abbatia. Having previously listed the names given to the "First Matter" by ancient authorities, the author now shifts to a personal promise of clarity, contrasting his approach with the intentionally obscure writings of his predecessors.</meta>

(86)

[Three horizontal rows of ornamental borders consisting of repeating oval and scroll motifs.]

**D**lthough I have mentioned much in my letter to you regarding the **blessed Art** <note>original: "geben-deytē Kunst"; the "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) of alchemy, aimed at the perfection of matter.</note>—without which, as *Raymund* <note>Raymundus Lullus, or Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher and theologian whose name was frequently attributed to alchemical texts.</note> says, the core of *Philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophiæ"; here meaning "Hermetic Philosophy," the study of the hidden laws of nature and transmutation.</note> cannot be perfectly attained—nevertheless, because I told you that, if God grants me grace to discover something, primarily regarding the **Red** <note>original: "auffs Rohte"; refers to the *Rubedo*, the final stage of the alchemical process which produces the Red Stone capable of transmuting metals into gold.</note> as I have discovered it for the **White** <note>original: "auffs weisse"; refers to the *Albedo*, the stage of purification that produces a "White Stone" capable of making silver.</note>, I wished to reveal this to you. For this reason, I wanted to attach this small addition to my epistle, so that you may perceive the complete truth.

I intend, therefore, to fulfill now with a full account what I have previously promised you; I will not be envious or ungrateful, as many before me have been. Indeed, one will hardly find anyone who has not described the **Art** in a hidden <note>original: "verdeckter Weise"; referring to the tradition of "Decknamen" or "cover-names" and allegories used to prevent the unworthy from understanding alchemical secrets.</note> manner.

By God! I do not wish to be numbered among them; instead, I have compassion for you, and therefore I want to explain all the **secrets** to you as much as is possible. I will show you how to make the **white** yellow, and the **yellow red**, which is the **secret masterpiece** of the **Wise** <note>original: "der Weisen geheimes Stück"; the "Wise" refers to the "Sages" or "Adepts"—those who have successfully completed the Stone.</note>.

Therefore, prick up your ears so that nothing which I say may escape you, for I will now teach you something that [has] never [come] from a <meta>The catchword "ines" suggests the sentence continues on the next page with "eines Menschen Mund" (a human mouth).</meta>

<summary>Antonius de Abbatia promises to abandon the traditional secrecy of alchemists and plainly reveal the methods for the "Red Work." He positions himself as a compassionate teacher who will explain the progression from white to yellow to red.</summary>
<keywords>Antonius de Abbatia, Albedo, Rubedo, Raymund Lull, alchemical secrets, Red Work, White Work, transmutation, Hermetic Philosophy</keywords>
