(87.)

F iiij

<meta>catchword: richten (to direct)</meta>

...[as] has ever been brought forth so brightly and clearly from a human mouth.

Therefore I exhort you above all things / that you diligently practice the foundational <term>sciences <note>original: "Wissenschaften"; in this context, the systematic study of natural laws and Hermetic philosophy.</note></term> / so that you may properly recognize / the beginning of natural things / as well as the causes and <term>elements <note>original: "Elementa"</note></term>. Also when and how **Nature** proceeds in an orderly and uniform manner from the essence of natural things through movement and natural causes.

From this you will then easily be able to draw a true understanding from the words of the **philosophers** / who have marked their books and speeches with signs / so that these might be a reminder to them / or so that they might encourage their descendants / to investigate this high and excellent science / which they have therefore described in <term>parables <note>original: "Gleichnissen"; allegories or metaphors used to hide alchemical secrets from the uninitiated.</note></term> / so that it might not perish entirely.

Thus it has now come to pass / that their books cannot be understood / except only by those who have made them.

Therefore some of the **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis"</note> have also said / that working according to the plain literal meaning <note>original: "klaren Buchstaben"</note> / is a waste of resources and the ruin of the **Work** <note>The *Magnum Opus* or alchemical process.</note>.

For this reason I exhort you / that you [direct] all your thoughts / which you have gathered from the books of the philosophers / or from your own head through your <term>imagination <note>original: "Einbilden"; the mental faculty of forming images or concepts, which the author warns must be grounded in natural principles rather than pure fantasy.</note></term> / toward the natural beginnings...

<vocab>sciences, elements, Nature, philosophers, parables/allegories, imagination</vocab>

<summary>Antonius de Abbatia encourages the student to study the foundational laws of nature rather than following the confusing, literal interpretations of old alchemical texts. He explains that ancient masters used "signs" and "parables" to preserve their secrets, making their writings intentionally difficult for the uninitiated to understand.</summary>
<keywords>natural science, elements, Nature, alchemical parables, literal interpretation, imagination, foundations of alchemy</keywords>

(88.)

...should direct / just as the **goldsmiths** do / who test the metals upon a black <term>touchstone <note>original: "Probier-stein"; a hard, dark stone used to test the purity of precious metals by observing the color of the streak they leave behind.</note></term> / by rubbing or stroking: so should you also test your opinions and imaginations against the natural beginnings / and confirm them with the foundations of **Philosophy** <note>original Latin: "Philosophiæ"</note>.

When you therefore come to understand the **Art** / especially when the sayings of the **Sages** agree with your imagination / then it will be correct.

And this is exactly what the **Master of the Art from Florence** <note>Likely a reference to a specific Florentine alchemist or Neoplatonist, possibly Marsilio Ficino, who sought to harmonize ancient wisdom.</note> wanted you to know / when he said / that the artist should hold the sayings of the **philosophers** against one another and unify them.

Thus also teaches the highly experienced and famous **Raymundus** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher known for his "Art," a complex system of logic using revolving diagrams to find universal truths.</note> in his **Art** / that one must lead that which is sought back around in a <term>circular manner <note>original: "Cirkkels-Weise"; a reference to the Lullian wheels used to combine different philosophical categories.</note></term> / according to the order of the general principles.

In which the truth of the whole being is recognized in no other way / than how the image of the one looking into a mirror / is seen.

In summary, I say to you / that / unless you first know how to bring all the opinions of the **philosophers** into one single opinion / and to draw all their labor and works into one correct work / and to set all figurative speech upon a firm foundation / then you must not stretch out your hands toward this <term>masterpiece <note>original: "Meisterstück"; the successful completion of the "Great Work" (the Philosopher's Stone).</note></term>. For the hands of those can accomplish nothing certain in the **Work** / whose...

<meta>catchword: Ge (likely the start of "Gedancken," meaning thoughts)</meta>

<summary>Antonius de Abbatia compares the testing of alchemical theories to a goldsmith using a touchstone, urging the student to harmonize the contradictory writings of past masters like Ramon Llull before beginning physical experimentation.</summary>

(89)

...spirit is wavering and unstable in its knowledge.

Therefore I say to you once more with repeated words / that / if you desire to know the **Art** / it is necessary that the properties of things / as well as their transformation be known to you / so that you may come to the knowledge of that same thing / from which our <term>medicine <note>original: "medicin"; in alchemy, the "medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals into gold and grant longevity.</note></term> is drawn / which cannot possibly happen without the will of **GOD**.

And in truth, it is among the deepest secrets of the **Art** / that one knows the right <term>material <note>original Latin: "materiam"; referring to the *materia prima* or "first matter," the essential starting substance for the alchemical process.</note></term> / which is closest and most immediate to the metallic genus.

By the holy Catholic faith / I cannot tell you enough / even if I were full of tongues <note>A common rhetorical device (polyglossia) meaning that even with a multitude of voices, the speaker could not fully express the scale of the problem.</note> / how many there are / who work in the **Art** / but not in the right material: no differently / than if they wanted to beget a human from a dog or a horse.

And if perchance some obtain the right material / of whom there are yet very few / they nevertheless mix it so much with other foreign things / that they ruin it and bring it to nothing.

Therefore one must leave them stuck in their error / for a camel would sooner go through the eye of a needle / than such people could bring anything good to pass. Therefore **Raymundus** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), the philosopher and polymath previously cited on page 88.</note> has said in his **Testament** / that such <term>sophists <note>original: "Sophisten"; here meaning "false alchemists" or "puffers" who use deceptive methods or incorrect logic rather than true natural philosophy.</note></term> can never attain to the knowledge.

<summary>The author stresses that the primary secret of alchemy is identifying the correct "first matter" that is naturally related to metals, warning that most seekers fail because they use the wrong substances or ruin the correct material with additives.</summary>
<keywords>Art, medicine, material, first matter, Raymundus, sophists, transformation, metallic genus</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 89, where the author emphasized the necessity of divine will and the correct starting material for alchemical success.</meta>

(90.)

Likewise also Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–813), a legendary figure in alchemy whose writings were considered foundational to the craft in Europe.</note>, a layman who was a master of all masters in the **Art**, set down these golden words in his *Summa* <note>original: "Summa"; likely referring to the *Summa perfectionis magisterii* (The Sum of Perfection), one of the most important alchemical textbooks of the Middle Ages.</note> regarding this same opinion:

> The seeker of this **Art** should not strive to invent a <term>sophistical <note>original: "Sophistisch"; in alchemy, "sophistry" refers to deceptive or false methods—using chemical tricks to make something look like gold rather than achieving a true, internal transformation.</note></term> goal for the work, but should always look toward the proper continuation of the work; then he will encounter the true **Art** and not be a sophist.

For our **Art** is preserved in the power of **GOD**, and He bestows it upon whom He wills, and also withdraws it—He who is highly praised and full of justice and goodness.

For perhaps **GOD** the **LORD**, as punishment for your sophistical work, might deny you the **Art** and plunge you into deviant error, and from error into wretchedness and everlasting misery. For he is very wretched and miserable whom **GOD**, after the completion of his work and labor, does not allow to see the truth, and who must end and conclude his lifetime in sadness. He is placed in constant affliction and surrounded by all misfortune and misery; he is robbed of all comfort, joy, and cheerfulness of this world, and must end his life in grief.

Also the Master of the **Art** from Florence <note>This likely refers to an alchemist or philosopher associated with the Florentine school, such as Marsilio Ficino, who integrated Hermetic philosophy with medicine and alchemy.</note> places this saying in the preface of his book:

> There are many obstacles to this famous science which hinder the artist, especially

<meta>catchword: especially [German: lich, the second half of 'absonderlich']</meta>

<summary>The author cites the authority of Geber and a Florentine master to warn that alchemy is a divine gift; those who pursue it through "sophistical" or false means risk divine punishment and a life of misery.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Summa Perfectionis, sophistry, divine grace, Florence, obstacles to alchemy, misery of failure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 90, where the author cited Geber's warnings against "sophistry" (deceptive chemical tricks) and emphasized that the Art is a gift from God that can be withdrawn as punishment for dishonesty.</meta>

...especially when he falls into despair and turns 
to making a **sophistical work**, which one must 
entirely guard against. The same author 
speaks at the end of the second part of the 11th chapter, 
stating that in the right investigation of this Art, all **sophistical labor** is rejected, and that it has no 
enemies but the **ignorant**.

Furthermore, the late **Thomas Aquinas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274) was a famous theologian. In the Middle Ages, many alchemical texts were attributed to him to give them authority, though most were written by later anonymous authors.</note> 
began his commentary on the **Assembly of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Turbam Philosophorum"; one of the earliest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between ancient philosophers like Anaxagoras and Democritus.</note> thus, from the 
mouth of the most wise **Solomon**: 

> My mouth shall speak the truth, and my lips shall curse the ungodly; 

and he says that in these preceding words he wishes to prove a 
twofold effect of wisdom, namely the truth of the 
**Alchemical Art**, which is a contemplation 
of the beginnings of nature, which is 
indicated when he says: 

> My mouth shall compose of the truth: 

and an over-
coming of the errors that are against the truth, which 
he touches upon when he says: 

> And my lips shall curse the ungodly, 

that is, the **falsifier** <note>original: "Verfälscher"; here referring to "puffers" or fraudulent alchemists who use quick fixes to mimic gold rather than true transmutation.</note>, and he adds the reason: since it 
is one and the same thing, regarding two opposing matters, 
to pursue the one and to refute the other: 
just as medicine does, which reintroduces 
health and drives out disease; 
Therefore, **Aristotle** in his *Sophistical Refutations* <note>original: "Elenchis"; referring to Aristotle’s *De Sophisticis Elenchis*, a treatise on logical fallacies.</note> has said 
that two things befit a wise man: first, that 
he brings forth no untruth; second, that 
he exposes those who do bring forth untruth.

<summary>The author cites Thomas Aquinas and Aristotle to argue that true alchemy is a branch of natural philosophy rooted in truth, contrasting it with 'sophistry' or fraudulent practices.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Aquinas, Turba Philosophorum, Solomon, Alchemy as Truth, Aristotle, Refutation of Sophistry, Falsifiers</keywords>

But let us return to our purpose. For you must now know that the actual **Matter** is obtained from that thing which **Raymundus** <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315) was a philosopher and logician. For they have given it so many names, and there is nothing that brings the artists into greater error than this **multiplicity of names**, though no one experienced in the Art has ever let themselves be bothered by it. Therefore, you should also not let yourself be confused, but always remember that it is **one single thing**, even though it may be called by different and almost countless names.

(94.)

It is not overwhelmed, but rests in it kindly and with joy.

We must not let this be driven from our heads, nor allow it to be interpreted differently than it is in itself according to the will of another who would instruct us; because, as you see, it is a work of Nature, which will by no means allow itself to be regulated according to the whims of our intellect. From this, now gain an understanding, if indeed you are subtle seekers of the Art.

Therefore, <term>Geber</term> <note>A foundational figure in alchemy, usually identified with the 8th-century scholar Jabir ibn Hayyan.</note> did not place the perfection of the <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: "Magisterij"; refers to the completion of the Great Work.</note> in <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphure"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle.</note> alone for itself, nor in <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argento vivo" or "Quecksilber"; representing the passive, feminine, and fluid principle.</note> alone, but says that each of them can enter into the preparation of the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapidis"; the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. For the volatile part, which Geber calls **quicksilver** <note>original: "arg. vivum" (argentum vivum).</note> because of its likeness to it, cannot be so hardened that it becomes a stone, because it lacks a fatty substance, which is a cause of hardness. Likewise, the fixed part, which Geber calls Sulfur, cannot be made into a stone by any art; and this is because it possesses no moisture within itself, by which the parts could hang together so dissolvably that they might be cast like a glue, without the destruction (loss) of the other.

From what I have said, it is obvious that both—namely the Moist and the Dry—are ordered toward the metallic nature, which is otherwise called a **dry water**; and this is the entire path to this masterpiece, and its perfect end. And this is known to all the wise in this Art: namely, that the material we take in hand is not made perfect except solely by means of <term>digestion</term> <note>original: "Däwung"; a process of slow, constant heat used to mature or "ripen" the alchemical substance.</note>. ...bound together, without that which is digestible <note>original: "verdäulich"; in this context, it refers to the alchemical process of *concoction*—the ripening of a substance through steady heat.</note>. However, that which is digestible primarily contains two parts within itself: a moist part and a dry part, as **Aristotle** confirms in his established description in the fourth book of *Meteorologica* <note>Aristotle’s *Meteorologica* was the standard textbook for medieval and early modern science regarding the formation of minerals and metals.</note>. Whereby he understands by the "opposing passive quality"—as it is commonly explained in that work—the moist which is mixed with the dry. For that which is actually and primarily digested in the moisture becomes dry through the mixing; by means of this, according to its specific proportions of mixing, it is concocted by the natural warmth.

The moist, however, undergoes no <term>digestion</term> <note>original: "digestion"; the process of "cooking" or maturing the alchemical matter in a sealed vessel.</note> without the dry; therefore it is stated elsewhere that water cannot be made thick through moisture alone.

Oh, how beautifully a philosopher has spoken: that these two parts, namely the moist and the dry, are called the "honored stones," which originally come from one father. They are two things by name, and yet in form they are only one, through which the mystery can be naturally accomplished—just as man and woman, though they are two by name, are yet only one in form to beget a son. And he is a true master who knows how to bring these two into one, until it becomes a precious stone—

(101.)
G iij
<meta>catchword: because</meta>

...undertaken to bring the same thing about anew, to which one can never attain, because it is a **work of Nature**, and is hardly produced by Nature alone without the assistance of **Art** <note>In alchemy, "Art" refers to the skill and labor of the alchemist, who uses human knowledge to manipulate and accelerate natural processes.</note> in a thousand years. Afterwards Art takes the same thing and works within it such that it shortens the work of Nature, and fulfills and brings to an end in a single day—that is, in a short time—what Nature alone could hardly do in a thousand years.

# ❦ (102.) ❧

...because of His mercy, He shall be praised and glorified by me forever.

The words of <term>Archilaus</term> <note>Likely referring to Archilaos, an ancient Greek philosopher sometimes cited in alchemical traditions.</note>, however, are these: O dear sons, you will find it very useful, both in the beginning and in the progress, to place the fresh water upon the common great lights—that is, the Sun <note>gold</note> and the Moon <note>silver</note>—since you will find three uses therein. Firstly, because our water becomes impregnated by the spirit of the <term>fifth essence</term> <note>original: "Fünfften Wesens" or "Quintessence"; the purest part of a substance, believed to be the celestial element.</note> of the Sun <note>original: "Solis"</note> or Moon <note>original: "Lunæ"</note>; these spirits <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> enter into the water until it stands over the body <note>original: "corpore"</note> of the common Moon or Sun. And when the water is drawn off from the bodies through a <term>helm</term> <note>original: "Helm"; the top part of a still or alembic used in distillation.</note>, the spirits remain in the water, which then dissolves [substances] more readily and more quickly. Secondly, the water also attains a greater subtlety and a better retention of the spirits <note>original: "spirituum"</note>, which do not perish once they have joined with the body. Thirdly, the Sun and Moon will also increase in weight; this increase can be enough for a <term>philosopher</term> <note>In alchemical texts, a "philosopher" is a practitioner of alchemy.</note> to sustain himself from it during his labor until the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis"; the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is finished.

Therefore a philosopher has said: You shall <term>impregnate</term> <note>original: "imprægniren"; to saturate or infuse.</note> the water with a body from which you can live until the Stone is completed.

And you should know, my son, that this water by itself performs wondrous things in alchemy <note>original: "Alchimia"</note> when it is applied to gold or silver; but when it is joined to its own body,

...so that it becomes a new *composition* <note>original: "Compositum"</note>; from this arises
an excellent and almost divine work, beyond which
one cannot go higher in Nature. These are the literal words
of *Archilaus*.

Likewise, *Geber* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century figure whose Latinized works formed the basis of European alchemy.</note>, who in his *Summa* <note>referring to the "Summa Perfectionis," a foundational alchemical textbook</note> clears away
all doubt in this art, spoke in the 28th *chapter* concerning
*sulfur* <note>original: "Sulphure"</note>, which applies here: By the highest **GOD**,
it illuminates every body, because it is of light,
and it is a *tincture* <note>a substance capable of staining or transforming the "body" of a metal</note>. And in the 23rd *chapter* regarding
the *medicine* <note>the transformative elixir</note> that makes the *Moon* <note>silver; original: "Lunam"</note> golden-yellow,
he says: "But if the *quicksilver* <note>mercury; original: "Argent. vivum"</note> should not
turn red—namely, by itself—then one should
take a portion of it that has not been 'killed' <note>stabilized or solidified</note>,
and it shall be *sublimated* <note>purified by heating into vapor and re-solidifying</note> several times
with *sulfur* until it becomes red like *cinnabar* <note>original: "Usifur"; a bright red mineral also known as vermilion</note>."

Do you not see now that *Geber* shows in these
two places as clear as day that the *tincture*
originates from our *sulfur*? And if someone were to
omit this, they would ruin the entire *tincture*,
just as I did when I made the white gold.

Note this well, my dear brothers, so that you
are not deceived in the governance of the *Stone* <note>the Philosopher's Stone; original: "Lapidis"</note>.
To that end, I instruct you so that you may
know how to be on your guard; you must read the books
of the philosophers much and often, and primarily
*Arnaldus de Villa Nova* in his great *Rosary* <note>referring to the "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous 14th-century alchemical treatise</note>, and
*Master of the Art of Florence*, especially in the 51st [chapter].

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of sulfur and tinctures into a new chapter that synthesizes the "Great Work." ### Chapter in which he examines all the labor of the Great Stone very astutely, and clearly explains it to one who does not have a delusional understanding.

Therefore, I conclude and say that the whole work of the artist is that he might understand and know that new <term>Compound (Compositum): a substance created by combining different purified elements</term>, and how we compose the same, by destroying that very humble thing—namely, the first compound of nature—through manual labor, so that we may obtain the two parts, the fixed and the volatile <note>original: "unfixe"</note>, from whose mixture the King <note>The "King" is a frequent alchemical symbol for the completed Philosophers' Stone or perfected gold.</note> is born, who sits upon his throne crowned with his crown, in whom are the <term>Sun (Sol): the alchemical name for gold</term> and the <term>Moon (Luna): the alchemical name for silver</term> and the other planets <note>In alchemy, the "planets" refer to the seven metals: Sun/Gold, Moon/Silver, Mars/Iron, Saturn/Lead, Jupiter/Tin, Venus/Copper, and Mercury/Quicksilver.</note> according to the desire and skill of the worker; and it is triangular in essence, and square in quality or property. <note>This refers to alchemical geometry: the "triangle" represents the Three Principles (Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt), while the "square" represents the Four Elements (Fire, Air, Water, and Earth).</note>

This is our highly exalted Stone, of which <term>Olybius: a legendary alchemical figure often cited as the discoverer of hidden ancient secrets</term> stated in his *Compendium* that its origin is two-colored, namely white and green.

Therefore, <term>Raymundus: referring to Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and polymath to whom many alchemical works were attributed</term> said that it has the color of a poisonous adder <note>original: "Eyderen"</note>; but <term>Alphidius: an influential Arabic alchemical authority known through medieval Latin translations</term> said that it is compared to the juice of herbs when first pressed out; and according to the opinion of <term>Arnold of Villanova (Arnoldi de villa Nova): a famous 13th-century physician and alchemist</term>, it has the scent of peonies; and according to <term>The Mirror (Speculario): likely the "Speculum Alchemiae," a foundational alchemical text</term>, it grows in the Red Sea <note>The "Red Sea" often symbolizes the final "reddening" (rubedo) stage of the alchemical process.</note>, and then begins to let itself be seen above the water when the sea is moved by the winds, and primarily by the south wind <note>The south wind was traditionally associated with the warmth necessary for the "ripening" of the alchemical matter.</note>, and leaps up high over the peaks of the mountains.

<summary>This page defines the "Great Work" as the destruction of a humble natural substance to extract its fixed and volatile parts, which are then recombined into the "King" or Philosophers' Stone. The author cites various historical authorities to describe the Stone's multifaceted nature, colors, and symbolic origins.</summary>

<keywords>Great Stone, Compositum, Fixed and Volatile, Alchemical Geometry, Sun and Moon, Olybius, Raymundus Lullus, Alphidius, Arnold of Villanova, Red Sea</keywords>

Likewise in the book of **Lully** <note>original: "Lullij," referring to Ramon Llull, a major figure in medieval alchemy</note> it is written thus: You must certainly not forget / that in our work / you need only one thing / that is our permanent water.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of the alchemical "Water" and the "Compound" (Compositum). it is to be concluded / that in our <term>Work (Werck): the entire alchemical process of transformation</term> there is only <term>Water: not common water, but the "mercurial" liquid substance believed to be the root of all metals</term> / which burns and makes white: it is Water / that dissolves and makes hard again / it is Water / that putrefies and afterwards brings it about / that various new things grow forth from it. Therefore / my son / I admonish you / that your entire intention should be directed solely / toward the <term>Decoction: the process of slow boiling or heating a substance in a liquid</term> of the Water / and do not let it vex you / if you desire to have profit and fruit / and do not worry about other vain things except only about the Water / cook the same gradually and let it putrefy / until it is transformed from one color to the perfect color / and take care / that in the beginning you do not burn its flower and its greenness / and do not hasten / to finish it too quickly. Also seal the vessel well / so that that which is inside cannot fly out <note>A reference to prevent the "spirits" or vapors from escaping through sublimation or evaporation.</note> / thus you will / God granting it / come to the desired end.

Furthermore you should know / my dear brothers / that such a <term>Compound (Compositum): the unified substance resulting from the combination of different elements in the Work</term> as made by us / as I mentioned above / has no name of its own / as the Master of the Art of Florence <note>original: "Magister artis de Florentia," likely a reference to an alchemical authority such as Johannes de Florentia</note> says / and if it had one / it would certainly be called by a special name by the <term>Philosophers: a term used by alchemists to refer to themselves and their predecessors</term>. And therefore you should take care / that you are not led astray / by the public speeches of the Wise / when they say / that our <term>Matter (Matery): the raw material or "Prima Materia" required for the Work</term> is sold publicly for a small amount of money / for in that case the Philosophers do not mean our Com- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "posi-", referring to the "Composition" or "Compound."</note>

[Com]pound <note>original: "positum," completing the word "Compositum" from the previous page</note>, which is brought about by our work from a natural thing; but rather they understand that same lowly thing of nature / which is also in itself a <term>Compound (Compositum): a substance made of multiple parts or "natures" joined together</term> / and has a composite nature / because in that same thing are found the things from which our <term>Compound (Compositum)</term> is composed, hidden under the cover of a mixed lump / so that under its cover and separable cloak it may be safe from the eyes of pursuers <note>Likely referring to the "unworthy" or rival alchemists who might steal the secret</note> / so that it might not be taken.

count / so that you might perceive what great harm ignorance brings / when one understands the words of the Philosophers <note>In alchemical literature, "the Philosophers" refers to the historical and legendary authorities of the Art, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber, whose writings were considered canonical.</note> differently than they were spoken by the Sages themselves.

Therefore it is now no wonder / that such people are scolded as blind by the Philosophers / since they uselessly waste their time / and miserably squander their own possessions.

Did I not tell you at the beginning of my epistle / that a human begets a human / from grain grows grain / from a horse is begotten a horse / from a dog a dog / each according to its own kind / and this comes from Nature / for she gives that which she has within herself.

Do you believe, you blind ones / that something can be made out of nothing / which no one but **GOD** alone can do. Therefore if you wish to make Gold or Silver / you must take an example according to the way / that Nature has set before your eyes. Do you not see / how Nature performs her works / and does not engage in so many foolish things. In these words / I have said almost more than too much / so that I even fear / I might be accused on the Last Judgment Day <note>original: "Jüngsten Tage," the day of divine judgment; the author fears he has revealed too many sacred secrets</note> because of such clear and distinct speech.

<meta>This page continues the author's concluding remarks to his "dear brothers" (fellow practitioners), moving from the social dangers of revealing the secret to the surprising simplicity of the Great Work once the natural principles are understood. It emphasizes that the true "Art" requires only one specific substance.</meta>

And I say to you / one last thing / my dear brothers / that this same <term>Work <note>German: "Werck." In alchemy, "The Work" (Opus) refers to the entire process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term> / which you seek / is not difficult to discover / nor does it require heavy labor / if only you will look upon the path of Nature / and upon the mineral roots. Yes, to say even more / and in this all the Sages agree / once Nature is revealed to someone / it is but child’s play / and women’s work <note>A common alchemical phrase (Latin: *ludus puerorum, opus mulierum*) implying that while the theory is difficult, the actual laboratory work is simple and requires patience rather than brute strength.</note>. And when you come to know this / you will wonder / that such a great / precious, and worthy treasure is hidden within / and that it is such an easy Work.

Now I have nothing more to say of this blessed <term>Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy.</note></term> / except only to remind you / that you should not trouble yourselves seeking other things / which may indeed have a beautiful appearance / but are nevertheless false / nor should you seek other materials / or means or medicines / than only this, our <term>Compound <note>German: "Compoſitum," referring to the specific alchemical mixture or the Stone itself.</note></term> or **dry water** <note>"Dry water" (*aqua sicca*) is a paradoxical term used by alchemists to describe the "mercury of the philosophers," a substance that acts like a liquid but does not wet the hands.</note> / with which / as <term>Raymundus <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and polymath. Many alchemical texts were attributed to him (the "Pseudo-Lullian" corpus), including a famous "Testament."</note></term> demonstrates at the end of his Testament / you will be able to perform such things / which the common man will regard as miracles / and believe that they occur against the course of human nature. And indeed, this is not only in metallic bodies / but also in human bodies / yes, even in plants / and in all everyday uses. You will also be able to make glass / so that it can be spread under the hammer <note>A reference to "malleable glass" (*vitrum flexile*), a legendary lost invention that alchemists claimed to rediscover.</note> / and you will be able to transform crystals into <term>carbuncles <note>A deep red gemstone, traditionally a ruby or garnet, often associated in alchemy with the color of the perfected Philosopher’s Stone.</note></term>.

<summary>The author concludes by encouraging the reader that the Great Work is surprisingly simple ("child's play") once the natural "mineral roots" are understood. He advises focusing solely on the "dry water" or "Compound," which can perfect metals, heal human bodies, and even create malleable glass.</summary>

Therefore I ask and exhort you, my dear brothers, that you would not be ungrateful toward **GOD** for such a great benefit, and do not forget to pray for my soul.

## Praise of Wisdom from Divine Holy Scripture.

Wisdom [of Solomon] Chapter 6, Verse 13. <note>original: "Sapient. Cap. 6. v. 13." This refers to the Book of Wisdom, often included in Bibles of this era.</note> For wisdom is beautiful and unfading, and she is easily seen by those who love her, and is found by those who seek her. Indeed, she meets and makes herself known to those who desire her. Whoever would have her quickly needs not much effort; he finds her waiting for him at his door. For to strive after her is the right prudence, and whoever is wakeful for her sake will not long be anxious. For she goes about and seeks those who are worthy of her, and she gladly appears to them on their way, and watches over them, that she might meet them.

Proverbs 3, Verse 13. <note>original: "Proverb. 3. v13."</note> Blessed is the man who finds wisdom, and the man who obtains understanding. For it is better to trade in her than in silver, and her profit is better than gold. She is more precious than pearls, and all that you may desire is not to be compared to her. Long life is in her right hand; in her left are riches and honor. Her ways are lovely ways, and all her paths are peace.

Job Chapter 28, Verse 15. <note>original: "Hiob. C. 28. v. 15."</note> One cannot give gold for her, nor silver to pay for her. Neither the gold of Ophir <note>a legendary region mentioned in the Bible as a source of high-quality gold</note>, nor precious onyx and sapphire, can equal her. Gold and diamond <term>Demand: an archaic German term for diamond, related to the word 'adamant'</term> cannot equal her, nor can one exchange golden jewelry for her. Coral <term>Ramoth: a Hebrew term for precious stones, often translated as coral</term> and crystal <term>Gabis: a Hebrew term usually interpreted as crystal or pearls</term> are not even considered; wisdom is of higher value than pearls. The topaz <term>Topasius</term> of Ethiopia <note>original: "Morenland," a historical term for Africa or the land of the Moors</note> is not esteemed as her equal, and the purest gold is not worth as much as she.

# Glory to God in the highest.
<note>original: "Gloria in excelsis Deo."</note>

<vocab>Wisdom, Holy Scripture, Wisdom of Solomon, Proverbs, Job, Gold of Ophir, Onyx, Sapphire, Diamond, Coral, Crystal, Topaz</vocab>

written that by which Your Majesty’s heart

so that you may therefore see, as if from a high mountain,
what is useful, and distinguish it from the useless.

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Edward Kelley’s dedication to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II. Having mentioned his imprisonment in the previous page, Kelley now adopts a tone of stern counsel, warning the Emperor that he has misunderstood the true nature of alchemy ("this work") while asserting his own integrity as a seeker of truth who identifies with the suffering of the righteous.</meta>

It would displease Your Majesty, were I to withhold from you the fact that you are wandering very far from the true purpose of this work, and are entirely losing your expense, time, labor, and hope. But I, who until now have been devoted to many and various arts, have finally learned this one thing: that nothing is higher, nothing more excellent, indeed nothing more pleasant than the Truth. Whoever does not respect it, it cannot be otherwise than that he must miserably remain in darkness and error <note>original: "Irzthumb."</note>. But it has always been the custom, and will likely remain until the end of the world, that Barabbas <note>original: "Barrabam."</note> is set free, while Christ <note>original: "Christum."</note> is crucified; this I have experienced in my own person, and I must content myself with it.

Yet I am in hope that my life and conduct will become so known and manifest to posterity that I may be counted among those who, for the sake of the truth, have always had to suffer much violence and injustice.

Be that as it may, the certainty and truth of this present little book will never grow old nor pass away. If Your Majesty deems it worthy to read in your leisure time, you will easily recognize that our intellect <term>Gedächtniß|In this period, the term "Gedächtniß" (memory) referred more broadly to the mind, cognitive faculties, or the capacity for intellectual invention.</term> has been occupied with such matters in no small measure.

<meta>This page continues the introductory section of Edward Kelley’s treatise, following the preface to Emperor Rudolf II. -phers <note>original: "philosophirer"; completing the word from the previous page</note> / have brought all things to their certain origin and beginning—namely, those things that are contained within the threefold distinction of Nature. To the animals, they have assigned procreation, which occurs through a mingling of the male and the female. To growing things <note>i.e., plants and vegetation</note>, they have assigned generation from their specific and implanted seeds. For the juices of the earth <note>original: "Erd-Säfften"; in the alchemical tradition, this refers to the liquid state of minerals or the "mercurial" substance from which metals grow</note>, they have established the earth and a slimy water as their beginning.

->2.<-

But in this general distinction, one should know that all other things which have a specific name—and which can be called <term>species|distinct groups or categories of beings sharing common traits</term> and <term>individua|singular, indivisible entities or individual things</term>—fall under these three categories, and each must be traced back to its own genus and, accordingly, to its specific beginning.

->3.<-

Therefore, whatever is a living animal must have come from a fleshly mingling. If it is a growth of the earth, it comes from its seed. If it is a mineral, it must have arisen from the gathering—or rather, the mingling—of earth and water according to its kind.

->4.<-

From this it is clear that everything is subject to a specific order and law of Nature, and by infallible necessity, must be classified and reckoned accordingly.

H iij

-> 5. <-

It follows, therefore, that living animals, as far as their origin is concerned, are distinct from plants <note>original: "Erdgewächsen"; literally "earth-growths"</note> and minerals. This is also to be understood conversely regarding plants: namely, that they possess different properties than animals and minerals. In the same way, minerals <note>original: "Mineralia"</note> are also quite different and widely distinguished from animals and plants.

-> 6. <-

Nevertheless, these principles <note>original: "principia"</note> or aforementioned things all share one and the same general matter <note>original: "Materiam"</note>, which is called <term>Chaos|the primordial, unformed state of the universe before it was organized into distinct things</term>—that is, a mingled lump or a mixed heap of all things.

-> 7. <-

This Chaos, which is otherwise called a disorderly mixture that extends very far, contains within itself the <term>elements|the four basic building blocks of the physical world</term> of all things. There are four of these: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth. Through their mixing and movement, all things under the heavens receive their form and shape, each according to its kind.

-> 8 / <-

These same things, as well as the elements, have four properties <note>original: "Eigenschafften"; in the science of the time, these were the basic qualities of matter</note>: Heat, Cold, Moisture, and Dryness. The first is attributed to Fire; the second to Water; the third to Air; and the fourth to Earth.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 117 regarding the four elements and their properties. While the previous page established that all things emerge from a "Chaos" of Fire, Air, Water, and Earth, this page defines how those elements interact to create physical form.</meta>

(118)

### 9.

Through these properties <note>Referring to the Heat, Cold, Moisture, and Dryness defined on the previous page</note>, an action and a suffering <note>In early modern science, "suffering" (passion) refers to the capacity of a substance to be acted upon or changed by an external force</note> arise among the elements themselves, and from this consequently follows a movement.

### 10.

Therefore, it is said that the elements act and also suffer among themselves, and they receive the names of the **Active** and the **Passive** <note>original: "Wirckende und Leidende"</note>.

### 11.

The **Active Elements** say: "We are those who give form to the passive ones, through a mark or a particular shape, according to how movement extends within every **composite** <note>original: "composito"; a physical body or substance made of multiple combined elements</note>." These we establish to be **Fire** and **Water**.

### 12.

The **Passive Elements** are said to be those which, by their nature, desire rest, and which subject themselves to every kind of formation resulting from the aforementioned actions. These, however, are **Earth** and **Air**.

### 13.

These same four elements also possess, alongside their active and passive power, a movement "before" and "behind" them (according to that which precedes and that which follows).

### 14.

The "preceding" and "following" are understood here in two ways: first, according to the position and state of the entire **Globe**; and second, for the excellence of its subsequent **final cause** <term>final cause|the ultimate purpose or "end goal" for which a thing exists, a concept from Aristotelian philosophy</term> in all things.

15. After <note>Catchword for the following page</note>

<summary>This page explains the dynamic relationship between the elements, categorizing Fire and Water as "Active" forces that provide form, while Earth and Air are "Passive" receptors that provide stability and rest.</summary>
<keywords>Active elements, Passive elements, Fire, Water, Earth, Air, composite, final cause, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of the four elements (Fire, Air, Water, Earth) started on page 118. It focuses on the hierarchy of these elements based on their natural movement and their role in the perfection of physical matter.</meta>

### 15.

According to the state of the entire globe, we see that what is heavy moves downward, while what is light draws upward. That which is of a middle property remains in the middle place.

### 16.

When a comparison is made in this way among the **passive elements** <note>original: "leidenden Elementen"; those elements that are acted upon by others</note>, Earth is preferred over Air, because it delights more in rest. For it is said that whatever is far removed from movement can always be more easily acted upon <note>original: "leiden"; in early modern philosophy, this refers to the capacity to receive an impression or be changed by an external force</note>.

### 17.

Concerning the excellence of the end <term>final cause|the ultimate purpose or "completion" toward which a natural process moves</term> of things, it must be said that this looks toward perfection or imperfection in this manner: the ripe is better than the unripe, but ripening comes solely from heat, which is a property of **fire**. Therefore, the active power of fire is preferred among the **active elements**.

### 18.

When this is thus considered, among the passive elements, that one also precedes the other which "suffers" the most—that is, what is acted upon more quickly and sooner. Therefore, in every **composite** <note>original: "Composito"; a physical body or substance made of multiple combined elements</note>, Earth is the first to be subjected to suffering <note>being acted upon/shaped</note>, and after that, the Air.

### 19.

In the same way, in every composite, the element that brings about perfection in its action...

<note>The text concludes here mid-sentence, continuing on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page establishes a hierarchy among the elements, arguing that Fire is the primary active element because its heat causes "ripening" and perfection, while Earth is the primary passive element because its stillness makes it the most ready to be shaped.</summary>

...is the last: for a progression occurs from the unripe to the ripe.

->20.<-

Since heat is a cause of ripening, then surely unripeness must come from cold.

->21.<-

Therefore, it is now manifest that the elements—within the chaos <note>original: "Chaos"; the unorganized primordial state of matter</note> of animals, plants, and minerals—are distant principles <term>principia|the most basic starting points or fundamental origins of a substance</term>. They receive a movement insofar as Water and Fire act upon them, and insofar as they are acted upon <note>original: "leiden"; to be passive or receive an impression from an external force</note> by Earth and Air. Within this, Water itself, while acting upon Earth, brings it closer to its own nature; in the same way, Fire, by warming the Air, makes it like itself.

->22.<-

Because every active power is attributed to the male, and the passive <note>original: "leidende"; literally "suffering," meaning the capacity to be shaped or changed</note> power to the female, the aforementioned elements are called **male** and **female** because of their acting and their being acted upon.

->23.<-

Therefore, every composite <note>original: "Compositum"; a physical body made of a mixture of elements</note> of the aforementioned threefold ladder—namely of animals, plants, and minerals—is called **female** insofar as it is Earth and Air; but in regard to Fire and Water, it is called **male**.

->24.<-

However, because only that which is dense <note>original: "dicke"; referring to physical density or gross matter</note> is subject to the senses, Fire and Air—which are subtle <note>original: "dünne"; meaning refined, ethereal, or non-corporeal</note> by nature—can in no way be seen within those things composed of the elements.

->25.<-

Next, two elements reveal themselves to us: namely Water and Earth. Among these, the aforementioned Earth is a container for Fire, and Water is a dwelling place for Air.

->26.<-

In these two principles <term>principiis|the most basic starting points or fundamental origins of a substance</term>, Nature has contained and surrounded all other things as if in far-off reaches. These the philosophers <term>Philosophi|the natural philosophers or alchemists of old</term> have, here and there in their teachings, categorized as male and female.

->27.<-

Therefore, if it is asked what the first matter <term>Materia|the fundamental substance from which a thing rises</term> of plants <term>Vegetabilien|the kingdom of growing things</term> might be, one must answer that it is Water and Earth, as they lie hidden in their seed, in which more Water than Earth is found.

->28.<-

In the same way, we shall say of animals that their first matter <term>Materia</term> is the seed of the male and female when they come together; in these, the greatest part is moist, while the smallest part is dry.

### 29.

Regarding the origin of minerals, one must state that a slimy water <note>original: "schleimig Wasser"; likely referring to a viscous, primordial liquid or "mucilage" from which matter emerges</note> came to a twofold earth, which we call pure and impure.

### 30.

The impure earth is called the combustible sulfur <note>original: "verbrennliche Schwefel"</note>, which hinders the casting <note>original: "den Guß"; referring to the ability of the material to be melted and poured or molded</note> and gives only a meager form to the water added to it. This can be seen in the lesser minerals, namely Marcasites <term>Marcasiten|in early alchemy, this often referred to base minerals or ores like pyrite that looked like metal but were brittle</term>, Antimony <term>Antimonio|a semi-metal; here used as an example of an "incomplete" mineral</term>, Magnesia <note>original: "Magnesia"; in this context, it likely refers to various mineral ores like manganese or magnetite rather than modern magnesium</note>, and the like.

### 31.

Pure earth is that which is so thoroughly bound with its aforementioned water that no force of fire can separate them; for they either both flee from it <note>that is, they evaporate or sublimate together</note> or they both remain stable within it.

### 32.

From such a slimy water and fluid earth or Sulfur <term>Sulphure|the alchemical principle of "soul" or "combustibility"</term> is composed that substance which is called Quicksilver <term>Quecksilber|Mercury, considered the "mother" or "spirit" of all metals</term>, which is to say, the first matter <term>Materia|the fundamental substance</term> of all metals.

### 33.

Therefore, metals are nothing other than a Mercury <term>Mercurius|the fundamental alchemical principle of fluidity and spirit</term> cooked through various effects of heat.

### 34.

These various effects of heat cause both the ripeness <note>original: "Reiffe"; referring to the maturity or "perfection" of a metal, like gold</note> and unripeness within the composition <term>Composito|the combined substance</term> of the metal.

It distinguishes between "pure" and "impure" earth and describes metals as mercury that has been "cooked" to varying degrees of ripeness by heat.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "first matter" of metals, moving from the elemental composition of mercury and sulfur to the concept of alchemical maturity or "ripeness." It defines gold as the only truly "ripe" metal, with all others being at various stages of "unripeness."</meta>

(123)

->35.<-

We call **Ripeness** <term>Reiffe|in alchemy, this refers to the degree of "perfection" or "fixity" a substance has attained through the metaphorical "cooking" of nature</term> that which has attained the effect and power of **Fire**, whether it be great or small, which we attribute to **Gold**.

->36.<-

**Unripe** <term>Unreif|referring to base metals that are considered "raw" or "cold" because they have not yet been fully transformed by natural heat</term> is that which ends in the **Element of Water** and has never endured the movement of the aforementioned noble agent—that is, the **Fire**. Examples of such are **Lead**, **Tin**, **Copper**, **Iron**, and **Silver**.

->37.<-

Therefore, there is only a single metal that possesses complete ripeness and rejoices in its own perfection: namely **Gold**, which for this reason is called a **perfect male body** <note>original: "perfect männlich corpus"; alchemists often characterized gold as masculine and solar, while silver was feminine and lunar</note>.

->38.<-

The others, however, end in their unripeness, and thus consequently in their **imperfection**.

->39.<-

But when a thing has reached the ultimate end of its unripeness, that shall then be the beginning of its ripeness. For the end of the preceding is a beginning of the following. <note>This reflects the alchemical belief in a continuous cycle of growth and transformation within the earth.</note>

->40.<-

**Silver**, however—because it has less of this watery unripeness than the other metals—is held to be somewhat impure, yet "fit" <note>original: "geschickt"; meaning ready or prepared for further refinement</note>; that is, its water is clothed with the hardening property of its **Earth**, which aims toward **Perfection**.

<summary>This page defines the alchemical concept of "ripeness" as the effect of fire on matter, identifying gold as the only fully mature metal. It categorizes other metals as "unripe" but notes that silver is the closest to reaching perfection due to its lower water content.</summary>

->41.<-

And this property is one reason why the aforementioned Silver is called by the Philosophers everywhere the **feminine perfect body** <note>original: "Weibliche perfect corpus"; in alchemical tradition, silver is associated with the Moon and femininity, while gold is the Sun and masculinity</note>.

->42.<-

Regarding the other metals, one should know that they are also distinguished in their **imperfection** <term>Imperfection|the state of being "unripe" or chemically incomplete according to alchemical theory</term>, depending on whether they reach their conclusion more or less within the aforementioned unripeness. Nevertheless, they all have a suitable beginning for **perfection** <term>Perfection|the ultimate state of a metal, usually gold, which has been fully "digested" by natural heat</term>, even if they still lack the complete hardening property of their said earth.

->43.<-

This same hardening power, however, is an effect of **earthly cold**, which is exactly equal to its own moisture, and is a cause of **fixity** <term>fixität|the quality of a substance that allows it to withstand intense heat without evaporating or changing</term> or constant hardness in the liquid **matter** <note>original: "materia"; the primal substance or "mother" of all metals</note>.

->44.<-

But the lesser metals do not persist in their fluid state within a violent fire (for they burn), and therefore they lack the perfect hardening power. That they nevertheless run together in the cold and become a hard lump occurs because the aforementioned earthly, lesser beginning is dispersed within them.

->45.<-

From this it is now to be concluded and follows, that the slimy water, when combined with its subtle or pure earth, brings about

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the primal "slimy water" or metallic seed, moving from the definition of perfect and imperfect metals to the practical requirements for successful alchemy.</meta>

...the mercury: from which mercury then follows—according to the opportunity of the aforementioned different **disposition** <note>original: "disposition"; the specific arrangement, state, or "mood" of the primal matter</note> and the attained property of some hardness (also by means of natural heat)—the various metals arise. Among these, two are called perfect: namely, Silver and Gold; the others, however, are considered imperfect.

->46<-

Therefore, whoever desires to follow nature in any way, in whatever manner it may be, must necessarily keep two things before his eyes at all times: first, that he takes that very same **matter** <note>original: "Materiam"</note> which nature uses for that same purpose. Second, that all his actions and works in the arrangement of the intended thing be either exactly the same or at least entirely similar to it. For **nature rejoices in nature, and one nature is improved by another** <note>This is a foundational alchemical axiom (often attributed to the ancient alchemist Pseudo-Democritus); it implies that to create gold, one must work with the "nature" or "seed" already present in metals, rather than using unrelated substances</note>.

->47.<-

From this, the error of those is revealed who attempt to extract the medicine to **tinge** <term>Tinge|to transmute or change the "color" and essence of a base metal into a precious one</term> the metals out of animals or plants. For because the tingeing agent and that which is tinged must be of one and the same origin and genus—and these same metals upon which the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: "Lapis philosophicus"</note> is cast are still imperfect during the application—it is therefore necessary that the mentioned powder of the Stone be, in its root or origin, a **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"</note>.

<summary>The author argues that all metals originate from mercury and that successful alchemy requires using the same "matter" and methods as nature. He specifically refutes the idea that the Philosopher's Stone can be made from organic materials like plants or animals, insisting it must share a metallic, mercurial origin.</summary>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Philosopher’s Stone must be derived from a metallic source, specifically mercury, rather than organic matter. It transitions from general principles to specific citations from authoritative alchemical texts like the "Turba Philosophorum" and Arnold of Villanova.</meta>

(126)

For as the *Practice in the Book of Truth* <note>original: "Ubung im Buch der Warheit"; likely referring to the *Exercitatio in Turbam Philosophorum*, a commentary on an ancient alchemical assembly</note> teaches in the first chapter, the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: "Lapis philosophicus"</note> is a metallic **matter** <note>original: "Materia"</note> which transforms the essence and the forms of the imperfect metals. But that such a transformation cannot happen except by its own kind <note>This refers to the alchemical principle that metals can only be "healed" or perfected by a substance that shares their metallic nature</note> has long since been demonstrated by all **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis"; in this context, the term refers to practitioners of the "Hermetic philosophy" or alchemy</note>. In order to prove that quicksilver, which they otherwise call **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium"</note>, is the first **matter** <note>original: "Materia"</note> of the metals, I will set down the sayings of several **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophorum"</note> here.

For first, the first practice on the **Crowd** <term>Turba|A reference to the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the earliest Latin alchemical texts, which takes the form of a debate among ancient sages</term> speaks thus in the first chapter: Therefore, from the consideration of all **philosophers**, **mercury** is a beginning of all metals.

The very same also says shortly thereafter: and just as flesh is generated from **coagulated** <term>Coagulated|thickened or clotted; in alchemy, this refers to the process of a liquid substance becoming solid</term> or curdled blood, so too is gold generated from **coagulated mercury**.

The very same at the end of the chapter: And all metallic bodies are **mercury**, both the pure and the impure, for they are generated from it.

Also **Arnold** <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist whose works were highly influential in the medieval and Renaissance periods</note> writes thus to the King of **Aragon**: But now you should know that the **matter** of all metals and their seed is a cooked and thickened **mercury** in the belly of the earth, through the boiling heat; and according to the difference of the **sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphuris"</note> and its quantity, different **metals** <note>original: "Metalla"</note> are formed in the earth.

<summary>The author cites authoritative sources, including the Turba Philosophorum and Arnold of Villanova, to prove that mercury is the "first matter" and universal seed of all metals. This supports the claim that the Philosopher's Stone must be metallic in origin to effect transformation.</summary>
<keywords>mercury, Philosopher's Stone, Turba Philosophorum, Arnold of Villanova, first matter, coagulation, sulfur, metallic seed</keywords>

begotten; yet their **matter** <note>original: "Materia"</note> is always only one and of the same essence, and they are only distinguished in an accidental way, namely because of a greater or lesser **decoction** <term>Decoction|The process of "cooking" or maturing a substance through heat; alchemists believed metals "grew" in the earth through a natural decoction</term> that burns or does not burn, or because of comparison. Ice or snow is turned into water by means of heat: therefore it was water before it became snow or ice. However, all **metals** <note>original: "Metalla"</note> are turned into quicksilver; therefore they were also quicksilver previously.

Through heat, according to its sulfurous variety, various metals are generated within the earth.

**Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and alchemy were foundational in Europe</note> agrees with them in chapter 3, where he says: It is certain that a thing is made of that into which it is **dissolved** <term>Dissolved|original: "resolviret"; the process of breaking a solid substance back down into its original liquid or elemental form</term>. ***The Sound of the Trumpet*** <note>original Latin title: "Clangor Buccinæ," a famous alchemical treatise</note>: Every **body** <note>original: "Corpus"; in alchemy, the solid form of a substance</note> that undergoes change is brought back to its first **matter** through effects contrary to its nature. ...opinion; that namely all metals are generated from the vapors of **Sulfur** <term>Sulfur|In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine principle of fire and combustibility</term> and from **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver|Latin: "argento vivo"; the common name for mercury, representing the passive, feminine principle of fluidity</term>.

Likewise, one finds in the same ***Assembly of the Philosophers*** <note>original Latin title: "Turba Philosophorum," one of the most famous and ancient alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a debate among sages</note> the following: It is certain that every thing is made of and from that into which it is **resolved** <note>original: "resolviret"; the process of breaking a substance down into its original components</note>. But all **metals** <note>original: "Metalla"</note> are brought back into a mercury; therefore, they were also mercury [in their original state].

Certainly, if it were worth the effort, I could have recounted a hundred sayings from the books of the philosophers for this proof; however, because this is sufficiently known to people practiced in this **study** <note>original: "Studio"; referring to the "Art" or "Great Work" of alchemy</note>, let this suffice for the confirmation of our project.

Indeed, many are in error, and even to this day many go astray, who prepare a cloudy and thick "water" of **antimony** <term>Antimony|A brittle, semi-metallic substance often used in purification; the author warns here that it cannot be the true starting material for gold</term> and the slimy matter which they **dissolve** <note>original: "dissolviren"</note> in a cellar or some other place beneath the earth—whether made from **sublimated mercury** <note>mercury that has been purified through heating to a vapor and cooling back into a solid</note> by itself or mixed with **tin** <term>Tin|referred to here by its planetary name "Jove" (Jupiter)</term> and **crude mercury**—and imagine that this is the **first matter of metals** <note>original: "prima materia metallorum"; the fundamental, primordial substance from which all metals are formed</note>. 

For as far as **antimony** is concerned, it can never reach the nature of a mercury or perhaps a metallic kind; and the reason is that its "water" and moisture are by no means mixed by nature with the subtle earthiness, nor does it possess the **oiliness** <note>original: "Schmirigkeit"; in alchemy, this refers to the cohesive, "unctuous" quality that allows metals to be malleable and ductile</note> that metals which allow themselves to be spread out <note>i.e., malleable metals like gold or silver</note> possess. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the purification of Mercury (Quicksilver). It warns the practitioner that over-refining Mercury into a transparent water destroys its generative power, citing the authority of the alchemist Bernard of Trevisan to explain how to properly clean the substance without killing its "metallic spirit."</meta>

Likewise Trevisanus: If that very water remains outside of metallic equality / how can such species be generated from this composition?

Those also err / who are of the opinion / that they want to extract a clear, transparent water from the **Mercury** <term>Mercurius|Mercury or Quicksilver, considered here not as a common metal but as the foundational "spirit" or "seed" of all metals</term> / and make many wondrous things from it. For even assuming / that they could produce such a water / such a work would nonetheless be useless for its nature and proportion: It cannot restore nor create a perfect metallic species. For as soon as the Mercury is transformed from its original nature / it is immediately forbidden from entering into our **philosophical work** <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>: because it has lost its **spermatic nature** <note>The life-giving, generative power of the substance, which alchemists compared to a seed</note> / and the properties of the metals.

<meta>This page continues the argument that the alchemical "Masterpiece" must be derived from its own nature—specifically metallic nature. (143)

<meta>catchword: Calid</meta>

Moreover, as Hermes testifies, nothing corresponds with a thing unless it is most closely related to its nature, and it shows in that thing a lineage similar to it: if you seek a medicine that produces metals, its origin must be from metals, because the species is tinted by its genus <note>original: *weil die species von seinem genere tingiret werden*; an alchemical principle that a specific substance (species) derives its properties from its general category (genus)</note>, as the Philosopher <note>likely referring to Aristotle</note> says.

So that we do not wander further in circles, we say that the whole **Masterpiece** <term>Masterpiece|The *Magnum Opus* or the Great Work of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term> is made through the union of Man and Woman, namely through the active and passive elements, by means of our metallic water and the required heat. This "Man" and "Woman" are indeed two metallic bodies. That they are two bodies, I will take it upon myself to prove in this place; and afterwards, I will also strive to demonstrate with very strong sayings of the Philosophers <note>original: *Philosophorum*</note> exactly what kind of bodies they are.

Therefore, one must begin with **Dancius** <note>Likely John Dastin, a famous 14th-century English alchemist</note>, who speaks thus: Prepare the bodies and dissolve <note>original: *solviret*</note> them.

**Rhasis** <note>Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, a Persian polymath and influential alchemical authority</note>: Convert the bodies into water, and out of that, afterwards make earth; then it is entirely prepared.

**Galen** <note>original: *Galienus*; the ancient Greek physician whose theories also influenced medieval chemistry</note>: Prepare the bodies and cleanse them of the blackness in which corruption lies, until the white becomes white and red <note>These colors refer to the stages of the alchemical process: Nigredo (black), Albedo (white), and Rubedo (red)</note>, then dissolve them both, etc.

<margin>Menabadus <note>A philosophical figure appearing in the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts.</note></margin> Much has been said, yet I advise those who follow that they should make the bodies such that they are no longer bodies, and make those things which have no bodies so that they may become bodies. *Avicenna.* <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and chemistry were foundational to European alchemy.</note> But their preparation is this: that you first purify the man from the one part, and the woman from the other part; for as far as they are purified from **filth** <note>original: *Unflath*; refers to the "superfluous" or "combustible" impurities in the base metals.</note>, they are better joined together, whereas if you do not first prepare the bodies, they cannot be united.

Trevisanus: <note>Bernard of Treviso (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist who spent his life seeking the Philosopher's Stone.</note> Therefore, the water does not dissolve the **bodies** <term>bodies|In alchemical texts, "bodies" (Latin: *corpora*) refers to solid matter, usually metallic or mineral, before they are "spiritualized" or dissolved.</term> <note>original: *Corpora*</note> / except only those that are of its own kind / and those which can be thickened by the bodies.

Hermes. <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy.</note> One should take the stones of mingling at the beginning of the work / while they are still fresh / and they shall be evenly mixed into the earth.

Speculum. <note>Likely referring to the *Speculum Alchemiae* (The Mirror of Alchemy), a work traditionally attributed to Roger Bacon.</note> It is necessary that our stone be extracted from the nature of two bodies / before a perfect **Elixir** <term>Elixir|The transformative substance, often equated with the Philosopher's Stone, capable of changing metals or prolonging life.</term> may be made from it;

Democritus. <note>Pseudo-Democritus, an early alchemical author (c. 1st century AD) whose works emphasized the sympathy between natural substances.</note> It is your duty / that in the first work you dissolve the bodies / even over the white ashes / and no grinding shall occur / except only with the water.

Arnold’s Rosary. <note>The *Rosarium Philosophorum* attributed to Arnold of Villanova, a seminal 13th-century alchemical text.</note> Therefore, from the natural things / which most closely agree in nature / one must necessarily collect the **Medicine** <term>Medicine|A term for the Philosopher's Stone, viewed as a "cure" for the "sickness" or base state of common metals.</term> <note>original: *Medicinem*</note>.

I hope / these testimonies will be sufficient to prove the multiplicity of the aforementioned bodies / from which our **mastery** <term>mastery|The *Magisterium*, referring to the completed Great Work or the secret process itself.</term> <note>original: *Magisterium*</note> is accomplished. But now I will also present in proper order / exactly what those bodies are.

In the interpretation of the Epistle of King Alexander: <note>Refers to a popular alchemical text written as a letter from Alexander the Great to his teacher Aristotle.</note> Therefore, in the arts which you practice / you should maintain that two must be married / of which one is the seed / and the other must be assigned to the wind.

But when silver is dissolved in warm water, it remains nonetheless fixed as before, and has no—or indeed a very slight—tincture, and yet it is ready and prepared to receive a tincture into itself, in the temperament of the warm and cold, and is called **Female Seed**, cold and dry; therefore their joining together is very fitting. 

There is also another reason: because gold and silver, each for itself, is difficult to cast and to melt, yet when they are joined together, they flow easily and melt away, as goldsmiths well know who solder gold together. Therefore, if only one of these two were in our **Stone** <term>Stone|The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical Great Work.</term>, the **Medicine** <term>Medicine|The Elixir or Tincture, which alchemists believed could "cure" base metals of their impurities to become gold.</term> would never flow easily through any masterpiece, nor give a tincture. I have refuted the errors of those who **labor** <term>labor|In alchemy, 'laboring' or 'working' refers to the practical, physical operations performed in the laboratory.</term> so badly and wrongly.

To this I have also added that the raw **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius*</note> is that same water which the **philosophers** <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the historical lineage of alchemical authorities whose writings formed the basis of the Great Work.</note> used in their **dissolutions** <note>original: *dissolutionibus*; the process of dissolving a solid body into a liquid state.</note>:

Afterward I proved that two bodies must be **dissolved**, and that these are none other than gold and silver: Now, however, I will in like manner bring forth the sayings and testimonies of the philosophers to show in what way both bodies must be joined together with the aforementioned raw **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*</note>.

> In the book *The Light of Lights* <note>original: *Lumen luminum*, a title of several influential alchemical treatises, often attributed to the scholar Rhazes or to Aristotle.</note> it is written thus: And you shall know that it is these very things which make white and red, within and without: namely gold, silver, and **Mercury**.

> *The same author later:* Further, I make it known that the dragon does not die unless he is slain with his brother and with his sister, and not by one alone, but by two at once. <note>In alchemical allegory, the "Dragon" often represents mercury or the raw, chaotic matter of the work. > **A Philosopher:** Whoever knows how to **tincture** <note>original: *tingiren*; to transform or "dye" a base metal into a noble one.</note> the **Sulfur** and the **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argentum vivum*</note> has arrived at the greatest secret; for this reason it is necessary that gold and silver be in the **Tincture**, and the **ferment** is its **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*</note>.

> *The Ladder of Philosophers* <note>original: *Scala philosophorum*, a famous 14th-century text detailing the stages of the alchemical process.</note>: Others say that the **Body** <note>original: *Corpus*</note> should in truth be added to these two, for the work will then be stronger, and its effect will be changed in a short time.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the composition of the Alchemical Stone, specifically focusing on the trinity of Body, Soul, and Spirit as defined by the classical authorities.</meta>

### The Treasury of Philosophy <note>original: *Thesaurus Philosophiæ*</note>
The **philosophers** <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the historical lineage of alchemical authorities whose writings formed the basis of the Great Work.</note> have said that our Stone is composed of spirit, body, and soul, and they have spoken the truth. For they have called the imperfect **body** <note>original: *Corpus*</note> the "body," the **ferment** <term>ferment|In alchemy, the 'ferment' is a substance (often gold or silver) added to the work to act like yeast, transforming the base matter into its own noble nature.</term> the "soul," and the water the "spirit."

### The Path of Paths <note>original: *Semita Semitæ*</note>
They have said, however, that our Stone is made from a body, soul, and spirit, and with this they have told the truth: For they compared the imperfect body to the "body" for the reason that it is weak; and the water they called a "spirit," and it is in truth a spirit, for it gives the life—which it did not have before—to the imperfect body, which is dead in itself, and brings it into another form. The ferment they called the "soul," because it gives life to the imperfect body (which is dead in itself), which it did not have before, and elevates and transforms it into its own nature.

### In the Same Place <note>original: *Ibidem*</note>
For the entire **Mastery** <term>Mastery|Also called the *Magisterium*, this refers to the successful completion of the alchemical process to create the Philosopher's Stone.</term> is prepared with and out of our water. For it **dissolves** <note>original: *solvirt*</note> the bodies, as has been said, etc., and it also **calcines** <note>original: *calcinirt*; the process of reducing a substance to a powder through heat or chemical action.</note> them and turns them into earth. It also transforms the bodies into ashes, gives them an ash-color, and makes them white and pure, according to the words of **Morienus** <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist.</note>, who says: that the **Azoth** <term>Azoth|The essential transformative agent in alchemy, often identified as the "mercury of the philosophers."</term> and the fire cleanse the **Laton** <term>Laton|An impure metal or "base" state of the matter, often described as a darkened brass or copper that must be whitened.</term>; that is, they wash it and completely take away its darkness. For Laton is an impure body, but Azoth is **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*</note>.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic trinity of the Alchemical Stone—Body, Soul, and Spirit—identifying them as the imperfect metal, the ferment, and the mercurial water. It describes how the "water" (Azoth) acts as a life-giving spirit that dissolves, calcines, and purifies the "dead" base matter (Laton).</summary>
<keywords>Trinity of the Stone, Body Soul and Spirit, Ferment, Magisterium, Morienus, Azoth, Laton, Quicksilver, Calcination, Dissolution</keywords>

### The Sound of the Trumpet <note>original: *Clangor Buccinæ*</note>

The **Elixir of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Elixir der Philosophorum*</note> however is composed of three / namely from the lunar, solar, and mercurial stones. In the lunar [stone] arises the **white sulfur** / in the solar [stone] the **red sulfur** / and the mercurial stone encompasses both natures / the white and the red / and this is the strength of the entire masterpiece.

### Crimenus
